INTRODUCTION 

"General Business Science" and the correlated "Projects in

Business Science" are designed to meet the recommendations of the Committee on the Aims of Junior Business Education as set forth in the Fifth Yearbook of the Department of Superintendence of the National Education Association. These aims are as follows: 

1. To give to students an understanding of those fundamental principles of business that are useful to all persons, whatever their occupations. 

2. To inculcate habits of thrift and to teach the means that are available to promote it. 

3. To meet a definite and increasing demand for business training in the junior high school without offering advanced types of commercial work to boys and girls too immature to profit by taking them. 

4. To fit those who must leave school at the end of the eighth grade or ninth grade for the kinds of positions that are open to them in business offices. 

5. To serve as a try-out course to the end that the student may know whether or not he wishes to continue with commercial subjects in the senior high school.

The committee also insisted that the subject be “organized and taught so as to contribute as fully as possible to the broad, general objectives of the junior high school."

The text and projects are also designed to meet the objectives of numerous state and city syllabuses in Junior Business Training. 

In addition, the materials are built upon teaching, guidance, and employment experiences of the authors, as well as upon experiment and recent research. Furthermore, both text and projects have the approval of modern business. 

The book is more than a text-it is both a text and a source book combined-rich in vitalized material, profusely illustrated, and motivated by projects that stimulate the student's interest. 

Many of the projects require the student to make a judgmenta decision in the light of his knowledge and experience. Many of them are intended for skill development, practice, and explora- 3

Introduction tion. They constitute an integrating or articulating type of material, and mathematics, history, English, and other school subjects are often injected into them. In other words, much of the student's entire cycle of previous education is focused on each project.

One of the greatest educational needs today is for a course in Business Science that fully meets the requirements of the junior high school-information, exploration, and guidance. The alert teacher will find in "General Business Science" an effective tool for the presentation of interesting business situations, for a study of business functions, for the introduction of students to the economic community, and for the presentation of the business aspects of citizenship. Such a course in business science may well take its place alongside general science, the social sciences, general mathematics, and other required subjects. To this end, "General 

Business Science," "Projects in Business Science," and this 

Teacher's Manual are dedicated.

LLOYD L. JONES LLOYD BERTSCHI

SUGGESTIONS FOR CLASSROOM USE 

In the development of the textbook and projects, the small as well as the large community and the small as well as the large school have been kept constantly in mind. A wealth of reading material and plenty of projects have been included and many suggestions have been made in order to give a wide range of choice to the teacher. The teacher thus has great freedom, and may select material best adapted to a particular classroom situation and to the specific needs of various groups of students. 

The text and projects have been prepared so that local business organizations and the business life of the community can be used to inspire the students to learn more about them. Why not use the greatest laboratory in the world-the immediate business community? Business should not be considered as something that is far removed from the lives of boys and girls, but rather as the ever-present institution that makes more and more goods and services available to more and more people. One of the primary purposes of the text and projects is to arouse the student to the realization that he is already an active part of the economic community.

A Well-Balanced Program. In order to achieve a well-balanced development of every student "as an end in himself," it is suggested that the teacher make full use of all reading material, illustrations, charts, questions, and business projects. In this way, the teacher will have a well-balanced, properly articulated program. However, the more the teacher can bring into the classroom in the way of experience or contacts in the business community, the more stimulating will be the classroom work. 

A high standard of work should be maintained throughout the course, and students should not be permitted to do slovenly research work on any of the questions or projects, or to fill out the forms carelessly. A businesslike manner of conducting the recitation will help to stimulate the students to do better work and more of it. 5

Teaching Plans for 

Illustrations and Charts. The illustrations and charts in the text are more than mere pictures. The captions and legends tie them up with both text and projects. Many of the charts are used as teaching devices and offer material upon which numerous classroom situations can be built, while others summarize and visualize the information given in the units of which they are a part. The use to which many of them may be put is apparent in the lesson plans that follow in this manual. 

Test, Research, and Discussion Questions. The questions for each unit are intended to start thinking processes, and are not merely routine quizzing questions. In many of them, the student is required to make a judgment, a comparison, or an investigation, or to state a personal opinion, preference, or choice, based on acquired information. In practically every group, there are three distinct types of questions, the nature of which may be described as follows: 

1. Test questions. These are based specifically on the subject matter of the unit. Answers to them will be uniform. 

2. Research questions. These require the student to consult dictionaries, encyclopedias, reference books, and similar sources of information outside of the textbook itself. Answers to them will likewise be uniform. 

3. Discussion questions. These are intended to bring forth statements of individual opinions, preferences, likes, choices, etc., and to provoke and encourage thought and discussion. Although related to the subject matter of the unit of which they are a part, answers will not be uniform. 

Business Projects. The business projects and the directions or instructions for them provide motivating material for the student. 

They offer numerous opportunities for exploration, and lead the student to an understanding of his economic community. Each project calls for independent work, independent investigation, and independent judgment. The project instructions are specific and must be followed in working out every project. This is good business training. It is good citizenship to be able to follow instructions; it is also the first requirement of an office employee. 

Every project affords the student an opportunity to demonstrate and improve his trustworthiness, his accuracy, his memory, and his powers of observation.

General Business Science 

Three-Level Assignments. The text and the projects are so designed that every student needs to work to his maximum capacity. There is enough work for the brightest student. The projects are really contracts, and provide for individual student differences and allow each student to go as far and do as much as his own ability permits. Each student should be urged to carry out each project as far as he is able, and each student should have the opportunity to do at least some work on every project. 

The projects were designed so that each one has a definite place in the scheme of the book. It is far better, therefore, for a student to work out only half of each project than to work out completely only half of all the projects. 

If the slow student works hard and steadily, he may be able to complete more projects than the bright student who does not work so steadily. The projects are designed to offer to every student an opportunity to show what he can do to carry out an independent piece of work-to proceed as far as his ability, time, and interest permit. Although there are many suggestions in the manual for the use of the text material and the projects, the teacher is free to determine exactly what constitutes superior work, an average grade of work, and a low grade of work. 

Instead of teaching the student as though he possessed inferior mentality, why not allow him a maximum of freedom to try himself out and prove what he can do? Why not offer the maximum of possibilities and opportunities to all students, even though the achievements of some may have natural limitations? After all, the slowest student will get some benefit from working at least a part of every project. If he can complete it, so much the better. 

This plan is not only a challenge to the brightest student, but also an encouragement to every student to work to his capacity, and the teacher is not compelled to give assignments that imply humiliating inferiority on the part of any student. 

Recognizing and Rewarding Superior Attainment. Adolescent youth is peculiarly sensitive, and the authors believe that better results will be secured from all students by providing equal opportunity for all and rewarding on the basis of individual achievement than by classifying students into groups of varying abilities and by limiting opportunities for achievement according to such a

8 

Teaching Plans for classification. It is a fact, however, that in many schools students are grouped by various methods into superior, average, and inferior classifications, variously designated as the A, B, and C groups, the X, Y, and Z groups, etc. The organization of "General 

Business Science" and "Projects in Business Science" will be found extremely helpful in meeting such conditions. 

In the first place, a number of the business projects are of such a nature that the privilege of working them out may be used as a reward for superior attainment on the part of students belonging to the superior group. Those projects that afford the student the opportunity to make personal investigations of various matters of a business nature by means of personal interviews, visits to business offices, etc., might be reserved for such use. Such projects as Nos. 19, 20, 26, 28, 36, 43, 65, 68, 77, 81, 96, 103, 109, 111, 130, 134, 142, 143, 144, and others, for example, might be reserved for this purpose. Any such practice, however, will, of necessity, deprive students not belonging to the superior group of just that much guidance, exploration, knowledge, and training. Yet it is these students of average or inferior ability whose need is greatest. 

Nevertheless, the business projects provide a means of varying assignments according to student abilities when conditions make it necessary to do so. The schedule of suggested assignments of business projects on pages 31 to 33 will indicate how this can be done under different conditions. For example, students belonging to the superior group might be expected to work out all the business projects designated as both "Required" and "Optional" in the five-days-a-week schedule. Students belonging to the average group might be expected to complete only those projects designated in the "Required" column of this schedule. 

The third group of students might be expected to complete only the "Required" group of projects in the three-days-a-week schedule. Many variations of this plan may easily be worked out by the individual teacher.

Still another plan, and one that the authors prefer, however, is to open the door of opportunity to all students to complete as much as possible of every project. If this is done, superior students might be expected to complete all of every project, average students approximately four-fifths of every project, and students

of inferior ability about two-thirds of every project. In this way, every student, of no matter what native ability, would have equal opportunity for maximum achievement, and each would be rated strictly on the basis of quantity and quality of individual attainment.

Vocabulary Building. Unit 29 of the text consists of groups of words selected from each of the preceding units for special study as a part of a systematic vocabulary-building program. In the days of experimentation with the manuscript, the question of vocabulary was given careful attention as one of the steps in bringing about a grade placement of the materials on the junior high school level. At the same time, every consideration was given to interest value, because the authors know from experience that the adolescent child possesses surprising ability to comprehend the context of a given piece of subject matter, provided it is interesting. Furthermore, much of the language style of "General 

Business Science" is definitive. That is to say, many of the words used in context are defined and explained in the context itself. 

This feature is especially noticeable in Units 13 to 28. 

Applied Mathematics. Although the students are presumed to know considerable about junior high school mathematics, many of the projects involve problems that can only be worked out by the application of arithmetic. The reason for much misunderstood mathematics is the inadequacy of explanation of the business situation that calls for the use of arithmetic. The business projects necessarily use arithmetic, especially in employment, transportation, shipping, communication, purchasing, selling, manufacturing, and filing. If business information is given and situations have been set up that are within the range of student experiences, most mathematics difficulties are solved. 

Legible Handwriting. Inasmuch as filling in the project forms in "General Business Science" involves handwriting, the teacher should insist upon a high standard of legibility and speed. Samples of handwriting of high standard should be displayed in the classroom. The teacher should have a copy of the "Gettysburg 

Edition" of the Ayres Handwriting Scale¹ so that the handwriting 

¹ Obtainable from the Department of Education, Russell Sage Foundation, 130 East 22 Street, New York City.

of students may be compared with it. Good notebooks in which good handwriting is used should be displayed and attention called to their superiority.

The teacher is not expected to teach penmanship, but he should insist upon good handwriting. It might be well to hold some remedial classes in handwriting. If the machinery of the school permits, the students who fall below the required standard might be sent back to some remedial or "hospital" class in handwriting. Insistence upon good posture, upon good writing materials, and upon a standard of not less than 70 to 80 upon the Ayres Gettysburg Scale will help to solve the problem of obtaining good handwriting from students.

Grading. Students should be graded and placed by means of teacher-made tests, which may be supplemented by the Objective Tests obtainable from the publisher. Many of the projects require the student to make personal investigations and reports, to express personal opinions and preferences, to use personal judgment, or to make decisions, all as a part of an underlying program of guidance and exploration. Such material is, naturally, almost wholly subjective. Satisfactory grading usually requires standardized or uniform answers, which, obviously, can seldom be given to questions pertaining to guidance. For grading and grouping purposes, therefore, formal tests should be used as measures of individual student attainment.

Testing Materials. Each well-conducted recitation involves the use, to some extent at least, of various types of testing processes. 

The test, research, and discussion questions at the end of each unit of the text, the additional questions suggested in the teaching plans presented in this manual, and those propounded by the teacher will all serve this purpose. Furthermore, most of the projects are, themselves, a means of testing the student's powers of observation and ability to follow directions, as well as his neatness, accuracy, penmanship, etc.

If the testing process is to serve its purpose to the fullest extent, however, tests of a purely objective nature are also required. 

Even though used for measuring achievement only, such tests provide the most satisfactory and reliable basis for determining grades. When used for diagnostic purposes in determining student

weaknesses and required remedial teaching, they become even more valuable. In recognition of the educational value of a definite, systematic testing program, the authors have prepared a series of brief objective tests, one of which is based on each unit of subject matter in the text. These are supplemented by two midterm and two semester-final tests of a more comprehensive nature. They have been made available by the publisher as a service to schools that use "General Business Science," rather than as a profit-making venture. 

Enriching the Course. The alert teacher of "General Business 

Science" will be able to devise many plans that will arouse the enthusiasm and maintain the interest of students, and that will, at the same time, enrich the course. The activities suggested under the following numbered topical headings were found helpful in the days of experimentation with the text and projects. These suggestions may, of course, be amplified and modified according to the wishes and judgment of the teacher and the circumstances under which the subject is taught. 

1. Individual Notebook. When the individual notebook is kept in an orderly manner, it is a valuable source of information for the individual who compiled it. Good penmanship and neatness are the first requirements. Into the notebook will go all the completed and approved projects. In addition, there will be clippings, business forms, essays, pictures, and other interesting materiala valuable accumulation upon which each student may look with pride. Any one of the standard loose-leaf binders may be secured and the project sheets punched to fit the covers. The plan should be modified according to the teacher's preference, however. 

2. Extra-Credit Materials. The student's interest in this course is often measured to a considerable extent by the clippings, pictures, advertisements, sample business forms, legal documents, catalogues, pamphlets, posters, time-tables, etc. that he collects from various sources. In many instances, the teacher may well require the collection of such materials as definite assignments. 

On the other hand, many students will extend their activities in this direction far beyond the requirements stipulated by the teacher's assignments. Obviously, the richer, the more appropriate, and the larger the collection made by each student, the

better that student's grade ought to be as a reward for his efforts beyond the ordinary requirements. By allowing extra credit for such activities, students will be encouraged to connect the facts they are studying and learning in this course with their everyday life situations. Furthermore, many of these supplementary materials may be suitable for exhibit purposes. 

3. Class Exhibit and Bulletin Board. When photographs, drawings, posters, and clippings are too rare for individual notebooks, they may be made part of the class exhibit. It may be found advantageous to assign committees to work out sections or units of the exhibit. By keeping such an exhibit over a number of years, a vast amount of valuable material and information will be accumulated and made available to students of the business courses. All kinds of worth-while current business information can be posted upon the bulletin board. In this way the business side of school and community life may be emphasized. 

4. A Business Science Library. Many valuable books, catalogues, pamphlets, periodicals, and other helpful materials can be obtained without charge from business houses, banks, chambers of commerce, business men's organizations and clubs, and many other sources. Business forms and small booklets, such as timetables, bulletins, reports, checks, notes, drafts, bills, orders, insurance policies, contracts, wills, deeds, mortgages, stock certificates, application blanks, and a great variety of other business documents may be secured from various sources at little or no cost.

Such materials may be brought together either in the classroom or in the school library for the use of the students. They may be filed in the usual manner of filing library books, or they may be preserved in small boxes, in files, or in convenient envelopes, when this method is more suitable or convenient. The preservation of business documents not only saves the time of members of the class, but it also avoids expense and trouble for the business houses and other organizations that are glad to supply them. 

5. A Bibliography for Supplementary Reading. The literature of business is so extensive that the opportunities for supplementary reading by both students and teacher are practically unlimited. 

Many interesting and valuable articles are constantly appearing

in newspapers and periodicals. The number of books on business subjects is already very large, and is increasing rapidly. In this, as in any other course, however, supplementary reading by students is desirable and effective only to the extent that it is well chosen and properly directed.

With that thought in mind, the authors have incorporated as a part of the lesson plans presented later in this manual a bibliography relevant to the unit of subject matter on which each lesson plan is based. The assignment of supplementary reading to be done by students is necessarily left to the judgment and discretion of the teacher, who will, of course, be governed by the availability of the materials or the ability to procure them. Materials suitable for students' and teachers' use are presented in separate groups. 

6. Cooperative Work. Such activities as counting tickets, taking tickets, distributing materials, sorting cards, carrying on a school student campaign, selecting school tags, indexing, and stamping can well be undertaken by the general business science class. 

In all such cooperative work, undertakings that have little or no educational value should be avoided. The teacher's judgment should always dominate in making this decision. 

7. Class Programs. The general business science classes can do much to assist in making programs for various occasions that demand some knowledge of both business and the community. 

For instance, the class might have charge of the programs for 

Thrift Week and for Fire-Prevention (or Insurance) Week. 

The class might plan programs for every day of Thrift Week and provide for the following: Thrift Day, Make-a-Budget Day, Pay-Your-Bills Day, Community Fund or Help-Others Day, Life-Insurance Day, Better-Purchasing Day, Own-Your-Own- Home Day, Make-a-Will Day, etc. 

8. Community Contacts. Visiting business offices and interviewing business people are effective activities for building an appreciation of business customs, practices, and procedures and of social relationships. In fact, it is the only way in which students can observe business people at work. The importance of making the most of such opportunities should be impressed upon the students.

Sometimes business men are annoyed by children who come in to interview them and to collect information, especially if no worth-while purpose is apparently served by such interviews. 

Perhaps an advance visit by the teacher himself and an explanation of the purpose of such visits and interviews by students will bring about an understanding and a desire to cooperate on the part of business people. Undoubtedly, some form of class organization will also help to solve this problem of making community contacts. For example, a committee from the class, or even a single individual, might secure information that the whole class could use. Perhaps superior work by some student might be rewarded by appointing him to do some interviewing. 

9. Teacher and Student Activities that Exemplify the Organization of a Business Office in the Classroom. There are many opportunities for the teacher of general business science to demonstrate the organization of a business office by organizing the activities in the classroom along similar lines. A businesslike organization of a class will not only relieve the teacher of many of the details of classroom management, but it will develop businesslike traits. in the students, help them to carry out assigned responsibilities, and teach them the division of labor and the team work that are necessary in any organization.

The following suggestions for class organization, built upon the functions and responsibilities of the modern business office, will be found helpful. An outline form is used in presenting these suggestions, so that the teacher may see the plan in its entirety and make whatever modifications may be necessary. 

I. TEACHER ACTIVITIES: 

A. The organization of each class on the same plan as that used in a fairly large business office. Each student in a class will be given a title, as indicated in Section II, "Student Activities," of this outline. 

B. The assignment of specific responsibilities and duties to each member of a class and the supervision necessary to insure satisfactory performance of prescribed duties:

Each student may be assigned the task of ruling up a certain type of record form and of recording pertinent information on that form. The type of record assigned to each student for this purpose will be largely governed by the nature of the duties and

responsibilities assigned to him as indicated by his title as a member of the class organization. For example, the student who acts as librarian might be required to rule up a form suitable for keeping a card index of all books, catalogues, business forms, etc. belonging to the business science library. 

C. A daily and weekly check-up on the performance of the duties of each student as a member of a businesslike class organization. 

This need not require more than two or three minutes either at the beginning or at the end of the daily class period, and not more than ten or fifteen minutes once each week. It will include checking the reports handed in by each student who has been assigned specific responsibilities and duties. 

II. STUDENT ACTIVITIES (the teacher may want to choose only a few class officers from the long list that follows):

A. The students of the general business science class may be advised to form a class organization. A constitution may be prepared, if necessary. The students may be promoted from one position to another by the teacher.

B. The students of the class may be assigned titles and duties as follows: 

1. Class Manager; Assistant Class Manager: 

a. Be responsible for collecting the reports from the various class officers at the end of each class period, at the end of each week, or at any other times according to instructions from the teacher. b. Preside at any class meetings. 

2. Class Secretary; Assistant: 

Keep record of attendance and tardiness according to teacher's directions and in the proper roll-book or roster. 

3. Delivery Clerk; Assistant: a. Distribute materials, papers, supplies issued by the teacher; keep record of absent students who did not receive materials. b. Deliver said materials to the absent ones when they return. (Perhaps delivery of the said material to the homes of the absent ones might be arranged for.) 

4. Stock Clerk; Assistant: a. Get materials, supplies, paper, etc. from the school supply room or bookstore when the teacher's supply is low. b. Keep a record with dates and amounts, together with perpetual inventory record card.

5. Record Clerk; Assistant:

Keep an accurate record of assigned, written, notebook, and project work, with dates, handed in by each student. 

6. Repeat-Work Clerk; Assistant: 

Keep an accurate record of work or assignments to be done over again—according to teacher's instructions. 

7. Make-up Clerk; Assistant:

Keep records of work made up because of absence or irregular attendance. 

8. Assignment Clerk; Assistant:

Keep records of additional or supplementary assignments that students have worked out and are ready to hand in. 

9. Reader and Correction Clerk; Assistant:

Correct certain kinds of work, objective tests, scoring, etc. 

10. Grade Clerk; Assistant:

Enter grades on work, record cards, or report cards from teacher's approved records. 

11. Appliances and Equipment Clerk; Assistant: 

a. Take care of any special office appliances, machinery, files, or equipment. b. See that such equipment is covered, closed, or locked up. 

12. Supervisor; Assistant:

Supervise actual performances and duties of delivery clerk and of students at work on specific assignments, such as filing, library books brought to the classroom, etc. 

13. Librarian; Assistant: a. Keep an index of all books, bulletins, catalogues, business forms, documents, and supplementary material. b. Keep a record of the students who have taken such material out of the classroom. 

14. Trait and Character Clerk; Assistant: a. Keep a record of special acts of courtesy among members of the class. b. Keep a record of discipline, discourtesy, violation of rules, penalties, etc. 

15. Inspector; Assistant: a. Clean blackboards, pick up papers, inspect the room, desks, etc., after each recitation. b. Arrange to have clean blackboards at the beginning of each period.

16. Ventilation Clerk; Assistant: a. Record temperature at beginning and end of each class period. b. Attend to ventilation and temperature according to teacher's orders or according to building rules. 

17. Illumination Clerk; Assistant: a. Attend to electric lights, adjustment of window shades at the beginning and end of each class period. b. Keep record of the kind of weather. 

18. Financial Clerk; Assistant:

Collect money for materials, supplies, equipment, trips, etc. 

19. Business-Contact Clerk; Assistant: a. Collect material, catalogues, bulletins, etc. from business houses. b. Interview business people and gather information. 

20. Correspondence Clerk; Assistant:

Write letters asking for information, catalogues, etc. Keep records of same.

AN EXPERIMENTAL OUTLINE FOR A TWO-SEMESTER COURSE 

While still in manuscript form, the various units of the text and the correlated projects were subjected to much classroom experimentation. The results of this experimentation and the best judgment of the authors are both reflected in the following experimental outline. Nevertheless, an outline of this kind is at best only suggestive, and it cannot reasonably be expected to meet all situations and conditions without at least some adaptation. 

Therefore, provision has been made in the outline, whereby the teacher may record the actual progress of his own classes and thus eventually build up an outline or schedule of progress on which he can rely absolutely, because it will be based on his own personal experience in his classroom use of the materials.

SUGGESTED ASSIGNMENTS OF BUSINESS PROJECTS TO MEET CURRICULUM VARIATIONS 

These suggested assignments are based on experimentation and are offered in the hope that they will be of some use to the teacher who is using "" for the first time. Recitation periods of approximately 40 minutes each have been taken as standard. Longer or shorter periods and variations in the amount of home work or supervised study will, of course, affect not necessarily the number of projects assigned, but rather the amount of each assigned project that can be completed. If conditions make it necessary to eliminate certain projects, some of those in the "Optional" groups should be selected for that purpose.

Although the suggested assignments apparently provide for five and for three weekly class periods only, they may be adapted to situations in which classes meet either four times or only twice each week. For example, if classes meet four times a week, some of the required projects in the five-day-a-week schedule might be made optional, and some of those in the optional group might be omitted. In a like manner, if classes meet only twice a week, some of the required projects in the three-day-a-week schedule might be made optional, and some of those in the optional group might be omitted.

The authors believe that in almost any circumstances it would be better for all students to do at least some work on every project than to complete only certain projects and to eliminate others. 

In this way students will be kept working at capacity, and one of the outcomes of this course will be a well-balanced development of every student "as an end in himself.” Here again, however, the judgment of the teacher and the conditions under which the materials are used should finally control.

FORMAL LESSON PLANS 

The importance of lesson planning is emphasized in many modern books on methods of teaching and in teacher-training courses. To say that a plan is essential to good teaching is almost trite. Although its importance is often minimized by teachers and sometimes exaggerated in textbooks on teaching methods and in teacher-training courses, a scientific lesson plan is essential to efficient, systematic classroom procedure. For the busy teacher it is a time- and labor-saving device, especially when using new text materials for the first time. Furthermore, it often serves as visual evidence of the teacher's knowledge of modern teaching practice. 

The following outline suggests a written lesson plan that meets with the approval of leading educational authorities. The numbered sections and the lettered divisions of each section indicate the essentials of correct classroom practice and procedure. Under each lettered division of every numbered section, suggestive details have been added in order to show how such a formal lesson plan may be adapted for use in connection with "General Business 

Science" and "Projects in Business Science." The preparation

and use of such a plan for each daily class period, or at least for each unit in the text, will be especially helpful in using the text and projects for the first time. 

All activities and processes that are a part of scientific classroom practice and procedure are indicated in the model lesson plan, each in its proper section. Not all these activities and processes will take place during every class period, but the essential features of the plan should be incorporated in the teaching of each unit of the text.

I. AIMS: 

MODEL LESSON PLAN A. GENERAL AIM (Primary): 

The general aim of the unit. (See "Objective" for each unit in Table of Contents.) B. SPECIFIC AIMS (Secondary): 

1. Immediate aims, such as the development of skills, knowledges, appreciations, and attitudes, that are a part of and that help to achieve the general aim. These are largely fixed by the text and project materials. 

2. Immediate aims that will be revealed only in the routine of he daily classroom procedure.

C. PROJECT AIM (see Project Titles): 

The aim or purpose of each project should be stated separately. 

Usually revealed in project titles, but should be amplified and explained when necessary.

II. MATERIALS: 

1. The text and accompanying project forms. 

2. Additional material, such as newspapers, clippings, books, directories, business forms, etc. 

3. Teacher's Manual. 

B. FOR THE STUDENTS (Primary): 

1. The textbook. Subject matter for reading, study, and discussion; charts and illustrations; test, research, and discussion questions; directions for working out business projects. 

2. The project forms. To be used as the basis of teaching situations in addition to being worked out according to directions in text. 

3. Other material. Clippings, pictures, advertisements, etc., from newspapers, magazines, and other sources; sample

business forms, catalogues, pamphlets, posters, time-tables, etc., from miscellaneous sources. 

4. Student's notebook. To be built up from completed business projects and such items as those mentioned in 3 above. 

1. Class library. Pamphlets, catalogues, directories, time-tables, reference books, business forms, etc., which may be obtained from many different sources. 

2. Class exhibit and bulletin board. Current business articles, pictures, illustrations, etc., of immediate but more or less passing interest to the class.

III. PREPARATION AND APPROACH (Teacher Activities): 

Review important facts of preceding lesson or lessons; questions and brief discussions by both teacher and students; summarize on blackboard if possible.

1. Describe a situation or tell a story that will show the application of student's existing knowledge to everyday life situations and tie it up with the thing to be learned. 

2. Exhibit a project form, a picture, an illustration, or an advertisement.

C. PRETESTS: 

1. By questions propounded by the teacher or rapid oral drill; in some cases one or more of the test, research, and discussion questions at the end of the unit under consideration make excellent material for the pretesting process. 

2. For skill if possible. 

3. Formal pretests highly desirable if teacher has time to prepare and administer them.

A. TRANSITION: 

1. The first paragraph or two in each unit preceding the first topical side heading has been definitely constructed to achieve transition and should be so used. 

2. Use both Old Knowledge and Common Knowledge as a bridge over which to approach New Knowledge. 

3. List some of the new topics on the blackboard. 

4. Present an appropriate project form; ask questions about it; bring the discussion to it; build upon it. 

1. Use freely the illustrations and charts in the text.

2. Show application of new knowledge to everyday life situations. 

3. Call for definitions of words and expressions ("Word Studies," 

Unit 29.) 

4. Use test, research, and discussion questions as models for others based on the subject matter under consideration. 

1. Student discussions of topics in text previously assigned for reading and study. 

2. The relating by students of their experiences in buying and using business goods and services, in employment, and in selling magazines, newspapers, etc. 

3. The relating by students of their vocational preferencestheir likes and dislikes for various kinds of jobs, with reasons therefor, their plans for the future, etc. 

4. Student responses to test, research, and discussion questions and to other questions propounded by the teacher. 

5. Student discussions of completed projects and of their personal experiences in working them out. 

6. Student reports on previously assigned supplementary reading 

B. ASSIGNED HOME WORK OR SUPERVISED STUDY FOR NEXT CLASS PERIOD: 

1. Reading and study of assigned topics from the text. 

2. Preparation of answers to test, research, and discussion questions. 

3. Working out of business projects as assigned. 

4. Assigned supplementary reading. (Invariably left to the judgment and discretion of the teacher.) 

5. Materials for next class period.

Visits to business offices, banks, factories, telephone exchanges, etc. may be made whenever circumstances permit. Written reports of such visits should be prepared by students and, when approved, should be filed in their notebooks. Such visits are in addition to those required by some of the business projects and are left entirely to the discretion of the teacher. 

Caution: Such visits may be made by individual students acting on their own initiative, but will probably be more satisfactory if made by the class as a group, accompanied by the teacher. Good judgment must be used by the teacher at all times in planning and supervising all students' visits to business offices. Carelessness in this respect is sure to result in annoyance to business men, thus depriving teacher and

students alike of worth-while opportunities for studying the economic community at first hand.

VI. TESTING: 

A. INFORMAL: 

B. 

1. Daily tests. These are an integral part of the Preparation and 

Approach, the Presentation, and the Assimilation and Application processes. The test, research, and discussion questions in the text and those propounded by the teacher constitute a part of informal daily testing. Filling out project forms is not only applied clerical practice, but also a test of ability to carry out instructions, to follow directions, to tabulate information in prescribed form, as well as of initiative, judgment, re liability, etc. 

2. Inspection of projects. Those in process as well as those already completed. (Many of the completed projects can be graded only subjectively.)

FORMAL: 

1. Vocabulary tests. To be given at end of each unit, or whenever the teacher thinks advisable. (See Unit 29.) 

2. Objective tests: 

a. At end of each unit of subject matter. (Available from the publisher.) b. At middle and end of semester. (Available from the publisher.) 

3. Individual reports. Individual reports, compositions, essays, themes, etc. may be required at the discretion of the teacher; students should be permitted to choose topics from lists suggested by the teacher.

1. Individual or group instruction, the necessity for which may be revealed by the testing process. 

2. Instruction that will achieve such additional aims or objectives, the necessity for which may be discovered by the teacher as he conducts the daily classes.

Sections I, II, and VI in the preceding Model Lesson Plan need no explanation, but some comments on Sections III, IV, and V may be helpful to the teacher who is using "General Business Science" and these lesson plans for the first time. 

Obviously, each division of Sections III and IV suggests teacher activities, whereas the divisions of Section V suggest student activities. The primary if not the sole function of a lesson plan

38 

 should be to serve as a guide to the teacher, and any attempt to make such plans function as material to be placed in the hands of students for any purpose whatever will tend to mechanize and routinize the recitation.

However, student activities both in the class period and during the study and practice period are merely a concomitant of teacher activities. What the student will do will be desirable and effectual only to the extent that the teacher's procedure is in accord with sound pedagogical practice. For that reason, Section V, which prescribes the student activities that are the outcomes of the teacher activities suggested in Sections III and IV, has been incorporated in this set of lesson plans. In this way it has been found possible to make the suggestions throughout each lesson plan more specific, and consequently more helpful. 

DAILY LESSON PLANS FOR UNITS 1-4 

To show how the foregoing model plan may be adapted and used in planning daily lessons in "," the following plans have been prepared for the fifteen class periods that, under ordinary circumstances, will be required for the completion of the first four units in the text. Their chief purpose is to show how the text and projects may be used in accordance with the best educational practice, to lay a track on which the wheels of classroom machinery will run smoothly during the first few days of the new term, and to serve as a guide for teachers who wish to prepare similar daily lesson plans for use in teaching subsequent units.

These lesson plans comprehend a properly correlated use of both text and business projects. However, the judgment and preference of the individual teacher, the length and frequency of recitation periods, and many other factors should always govern classroom activities. Hence, these plans are offered simply to help in the expression of the teacher's individuality and freedom. 

No doubt, most teachers will prefer to prepare their own lesson plans even for these first four units. For this purpose, the seven major sections of the model lesson plan and the lettered subdivisions of each section may be typed on sheets of ordinary

letter-size paper. Each division of the various sections may then be filled in with such details for each daily recitation as may be deemed necessary. The plan should, of course, be kept in a looseleaf or other suitable binder.

Lesson plans prepared in this way may be used as a diary or log book for each recitation. Comments and criticisms may be noted in each plan, which will be helpful in bringing about more effective teaching of the same material on future occasions. In a like manner, the teacher can check up on the results of a recitation as one of the preliminary steps in preparing for the next succeeding recitation. For this purpose, a v-shaped check mark (√) opposite an item in a lesson plan might indicate satisfaction with both presentation and assimilation of that item; a cross-mark (X) opposite an item might indicate dissatisfaction with the assimilation by students and the need for further emphasis during the next recitation; a circle (O) might indicate the need for more effective presentation; a square (□) might indicate an omitted item that needs to be brought up during the next class period. 

I. AIMS: 

UNIT 1-THE FIRST CLASS PERIOD  

To explain the purpose of this course in business science.

(See 

"Objective," Unit 2, Table of Contents, page ix.) B. SPECIFIC AIM: 

To introduce the students to the new textbook and project pads, and to list some facts about them.

II. MATERIALS: 

1. Desk copies of "General Business Science" and "Projects in

Business Science," Parts I and II. 

2. The Teacher's Manual. 

B. FOR THE STUDENTS (Primary): 

1. The text, "General Business Science," and the project pad, "Projects in Business Science," Part I. 

2. Paper either notebook or plain.

III. PREPARATION AND APPROACH (Teacher Activities): 

As this is the first class period, students will have had no opportunity to acquire old knowledge from previous study of the subject.

1. Point out briefly that students already live in a business community and are users or consumers of business goods and services. Business supplies the food, clothing, shelter and other necessities, comforts, conveniences, and luxuries that people use in everyday life. Using these two facts as a starting point, have students relate personal experiences in buying and using business goods and services. Develop the fact that this course in business science deals with a subject that is now, and always will be, a part of the everyday life of every student. 

2. Call attention to the picture on page viii. Use the school buildings as an illustration of one of the things that America does to provide better opportunities for its citizens. 

C. PRETEST: 

Inasmuch as this is the first class period, this activity will naturally be restricted to such questions as the teacher may propound. Test, research, and discussion questions on page 9 may be used (except No. 3).

A. TRANSITION: 

1. Use the first two paragraphs of Unit 1. 

2. Call on students to mention some of the things they would like to learn in this course in business science, why they are taking the course, what their plans are for the future, etc. 

3. Call attention to the titles of the textbook and project pad and to the names of the authors and publisher, as facts to be gleaned from examining the materials to be used in the course. 

4. Develop the fact that these materials are available for use by students only because business has made them so. Lumbering, paper manufacturing, cotton growing, cloth weaving, ink manufacturing, printing, binding, transportation, etc., are a few examples of business activities that have helped to place the finished book in the hands of students. Even the school building, desks, blackboards, and other equipment have been provided by business enterprises. 

5. Point out the twofold purpose of this course, which is: (a) to acquire the business knowledge that everyone needs regardless of his occupation, and (b) to acquire the knowledge, training,

and skill needed by clerical workers in business offices. Doctors, lawyers, teachers, engineers, housewives and mothers, students, etc. all use business goods and services and need to know how to use them intelligently and efficiently; business firms and their employees likewise need to understand and appreciate the consumer or customer point of view. 

6. Call attention: (a) to the Table of Contents, and (b) to the 

"theme song" of the course, as stated on page 1, "Essential

Business Knowledge and Practice for both Consumer and 

Business Worker."

1. Present the index of the book as a summary of what the whole course is about and call attention to some of the more important headings. Looking up information and using an index are common duties of business workers. Such things must be done with speed and accuracy. 

2. Open the text to page 3. Say something like the following: "This course in business science will take us on a journey through many great fields of business activity, such as Transportation, Communication, Finance and Banking, Insurance, Production, and Distribution. On the journey we shall see how these great business activities supply us with the things we need and want; what opportunities for employment and promotion there are in the different fields of business; how to go about preparing for such employment; and how to use intelligently and efficiently the different goods and services supplied by business. We shall also have many opportunities to try out different kinds of work so that we can find out whether or not we like to do them." 

3. Point to the chart, "The Door to Achievement is Open to All," on page 2. Explain briefly how the three levels of occupation, of preparation, of achievement, and of reward go hand in hand. 

4. Call attention to the title of Unit 1, to the topical headings in the unit, and to the pictures on pages 7 and 8. The drawing on page 8 is especially important. 

5. Call attention to "Instructions to the Student" on page 2 of "Projects in Business Science," Part I. At this time students may well be instructed to secure some kind of loose-leaf binders for use in building up their individual notebooks. 

6. Call attention to the form for Business Project No. 1 and to the instructions on page 9 of the text for working out that project. 

This project will give students an opportunity to learn something about the business enterprises in their own community.

(These processes will necessarily be restricted during the first class period.)

1. Student activities to consist largely of carrying out the suggestions made in Sections III and IV above, namely, relating experiences of a personal nature; expressing vocational preferences and plans; examining the text and project pad; gleaning facts about the title, the authors, and the publisher; 

consulting the Table of Contents and the Index; examining illustrations, charts, etc.;

and reading the title and topical headings of Unit 1. 

2. Descriptions by students of their personal experiences in buying clothing, food, school supplies, and other things that business provides. (See III-B-1 above.) 

3. Statements by students of the things they would like to learn in this course, their reasons for taking the course, their plans for the future, etc. (See IV-A-2 above.) 

4. Statements by students of facts about the title, publisher, authors, Table of Contents, etc. of the textbook and project pad. (See IV-A-4, 5, and 6 above.) 

5. Reading "Instructions to the Student" on page 2 of "Projects in Business Science," Part I. (See IV-B-5 above.)

B. ASSIGNED HOME WORK OR SUPERVISED STUDY FOR THE NEXT CLASS PERIOD: 

1. Read Unit 1 as far as test, research, and discussion questions, page 9. 

2. Supplementary reading from bibliography, page 50, or from other sources at teacher's discretion. 

3. Materials for next class period: the textbook and project pad. 

None. 

VI. TESTING: 

A. INFORMAL: 

This will, of course, be limited during the first class period, and will consist only of such questions as the teacher may see fit to ask.

B. FORMAL: 

None.

I. AIMS: 

UNIT 1-THE SECOND CLASS PERIOD  

To introduce the student to the opportunities and responsibilities of life.

1. To show the need for business knowledge. 

2. To help the student see that he is already a user or consumer of business goods and services. 

3. To help the student see that the business community is close to everyone.

C. PROJECT AIMS (see Project Title): 

To gather information about the student's business community.

II. MATERIALS: 

1. The text and project pads. 

2. A newspaper and a telephone directory. 

3. The Teacher's Manual. 

B. FOR THE STUDENTS (Primary): 

The textbook and project pad.

III. PREPARATION AND APPROACH (Teacher Activities): 

By questions and rapid oral drill, review the facts developed in the preceding class period.

1. Point out that, in addition to business goods, such as food and clothing, we are already using many business services, such as those provided by newspaper and magazine publishers, telephone companies, street-car companies, etc. For the time being, restrict references to business goods and services to those commonly used by students, who should be called upon to relate personal experiences in the use of familiar business services. 

2. Exhibit the newspaper and telephone directory and explain how widely these familiar things are used in the daily lives of almost everybody. Read a few items of a business nature from the newspaper and quote a few items of business information to be found in the telephone directory.

A. TRANSITION: 

1. The story of wheat from seed to bread or from farm to table will show the importance of many different business activities in the production and distribution of foods for the final benefit of the user or consumer. Every person engaged in any of the activities involved needs business knowledge. 

2. Turn to the chart, “The Door to Achievement is Open to All," on page 2. Have some student read the chart to the class and discuss it. 

3. List some of the more important topics of Unit 1 on the board and ask a few thought- and discussion-provoking questions about them.

1. Call attention to the fact that Unit 1 deals with the business community and with the student's place in his own immediate community. Ask, Is there any person or family that does not deal with business every day? 

2. Ask questions such as these: What kinds of stores and other business organizations are there in your community? What does each sell? Who are the buyers? What business functions are performed by retail stores; by factories; by newspaper and magazine publishers; by telephone companies; by street-car companies? 

3. Name ten business concerns in your community. What does each sell? Name some articles that are bought by the following: a school, a church, a school athletic association, a movingpicture theater. (List these items on the blackboard.) 

4. Use the first word study from Unit 29 as a definite wordbuilding feature of this lesson. 

5. Have students turn to the illustration on page 8 of the text and ask them to name the different kinds of businesses represented in the drawing. Why does a business concern want to know about the business community? Why should users or consumers of business goods and services know about it? Numerous other questions based on the content of the unit should be asked.

1. Brief summary by students of previous lesson. (See III-A 

above.) 

2. Students' stories of personal experiences in the use of familiar business services; preparation of a brief list of firms that supply common business services. (See III-B-1 above.)

3. Reading by students of a brief item of a business nature from a newspaper and mentioning kinds of business information found in the telephone directory. 

4. Answers to questions suggested in IV-A-3 and B-1, 2, and 3 above. 

5. A story by some student similar to that suggested in IV-A-1 above, using some familiar article. 

6. Discussion by students of the chart, "The Door to Achievement is Open to All," on page 2 of the text. (See IV-A-2 above.) 

7. Students' pronunciation, definition, and possible use of words in vocabulary study for this unit. (See IV-B-4 above.)

B. ASSIGNED HOME WORK OR SUPERVISED STUDY FOR NEXT CLASS PERIOD: 

1. Test, research, and discussion questions 1, 2, and 3, page 9. 

Note: Explain the threefold nature of the test, research, and discussion questions. The answers to test questions will always be found in the unit on which they are based. To find the answers to research questions, the student must consult other sources of information. Answers to both test and research questions will be definite, specific, and uniform. The answers to discussion questions will be in the nature of expressions of individual opinions, preferences, judgments, choices, etc.

Telling about personal experiences is especially to be encouraged. Obviously, responses to discussion questions will not be uniform. 

2. Completion of first five items of Business Project No. 1. 

3. Materials for next class period: the textbook and project pad.

VI. TESTING: 

A. INFORMAL: 

I. AIMS: 

The informal testing process for this class period consists of such informal questions as those suggested in Sections III and IV above and such additional questions as the teacher may ask. 

Note: It will be observed that the lesson plan for this class period does not include all the activities and processes mentioned in the model lesson plan. Most of those omitted here, however, occur in the succeeding plans for the remaining class periods to be devoted to Unit 1.

UNIT 1-THE THIRD CLASS PERIOD  

To introduce the student to the opportunities and responsibilities of life (continued).

1. To elicit student responses to test, research, and discussion questions 1, 2, and 3. 

2. To develop student attitudes indicated by the topical headings, "The Door Is Open," page 4; "Knowing about Life," page 5; "Self-Dependence," pages 5-6; "The Business Community, pages 6-7; and "What the Consumer Should Know about His 

Community," pages 7-8. 

3. To encourage student discussions of and reactions to subject matter of the unit.

C. PROJECT AIM: 

To discuss information gathered by students in working out the first five items in Business Project No. 1. 

II. MATERIALS: 

The textbook and project pad.

B. FOR THE STUDENTS (Primary): 

The textbook and project pad.

III. PREPARATION AND APPROACH (Teacher Activities): 

1. By questions and rapid oral drill review the subject matter of the unit that was assigned for home work and supervised study in the first class period. 

2. Encourage discussion by students and try to get expressions of opinions from them about the topics treated in the unit. 

Ask the students how they came to school this morningwhether they walked or rode. Those who rode used transportation service of some kind, and this service is available to the people in the community only because some business enterprise supplies it. Expand this idea and encourage the students to relate personal experiences in the use of transportation services.

A. TRANSITION: 

Call attention to the part that transportation plays in the numerous activities involved in supplying some article of clothing, such as a pair of shoes or a woolen coat, for example. 

Hides from Argentina and wool from Australia can be brought into this country, made into shoes or clothing, and distributed to the user or consumer only because transportation facilities exist. (Development of appreciation and desirable attitudes.)

1.

Student responses to test, research, and discussion questions 1, 2, and 3. 

2. Student discussion of the information revealed by the data gathered in working out the first five items of Business Project 

No. 1. 

1. Students' summary of previous lesson. (See III-A-1 above.) 

2. The relating by students of their personal experiences in the use of various transportation services. 

3. Answers to questions 1, 2, and 3. (See IV-B-1 above.) 

4. Discussion by students of Project No. 1 and the relating of their experiences in obtaining the information for the first five items.

B. ASSIGNED HOME WORK OR SUPERVISED STUDY FOR NEXT CLASS PERIOD: 

1. Test, research, and discussion questions 4 and 5, page 9.

2. Complete the remainder of Business Project No. 1. 

3. Materials for next class period: the text and project pad.

VI. TESTING: 

A. INFORMAL: 

I. AIMS: 

The informal testing process for this class period consists of the first three test, research, and discussion questions and such additional questions as the teacher may ask.

UNIT 1-THE FOURTH CLASS PERIOD  

To introduce the student to the opportunities and responsibilities of life (continued). 

B. SPECIFIC AIM: 

To develop answers to test, research, and discussion questions 4 and 5, and to stimulate student discussions of the business community, personal experiences, etc.

C. PROJECT AIM: 

To discuss the remainder of Business Project No. 1, and to emphasize that the student is an active, vital part of the business community; i.e., to arouse his business-community consciousness. He is a user or consumer of business goods and services.

II. MATERIALS: 

The textbook and project pad.

B. FOR THE STUDENTS: 

The textbook and project pad.

III. PREPARATION AND APPROACH (Teacher Activities): 

Rapid review, drill, and discussion of subject matter of Unit 1.

The relating by students of personal experiences in the use of the more common business goods and services. 

A. TRANSITION: 

Call attention to the fact that Business Project No. 1 is a business document similar to those used by business concerns in making studies of business communities for business purposes.

1. Have students give their answers to questions 4 and 5. List some of the different kinds of businesses mentioned in answers to question 4 on the board. Do the same with the goods and services mentioned in answers to question 5 and encourage student discussions, the relating of personal experiences, etc. 

2. Call on several students to give the information they have gathered in working out Project No. 1. Encourage each to relate personal experiences in gathering this information.

2. Answers to questions 4 and 5. (See IV-B-1 above.)

Many students who have not actually had such experiences themselves will be able to tell about experiences of parents, relatives, or friends. (See III-B above.)

1. Read Unit 2 as far as "Finding Out about Occupations," page 15.

In this unit, none have been made, although students may have made several personal investigations in order to supply information needed in order to complete Project No. 1. 

No written reports.

VI. TESTING: 

A. INFORMAL: 

1. Get student responses to test, research, and discussion questions included in the recitation for the day. 

2. Have students check the information in items 4, 5, 6, and 10 of

Project No. 1. The information in the other sections of this project will not be uniform. 

Note: The first five minutes of each class period may well be spent by the teacher, with roll book in hand, making a brief inspection of completed projects for the day, the student's notebook, etc. For this purpose, each student should be required to open his project pad to the project under consideration for the day. By walking rapidly up and down the aisles the teacher can quickly inspect each student's work. 

Frequent suggestions to students about handwriting, neatness, etc., will pay big dividends and relieve the teacher of much of the drudgery of checking and evaluating completed projects. 

3. Three-level attainment. It should be remembered that any attempt to grade the completed business projects in this course must be purely subjective. In grading projects, the following might well be used as a standard: a. A grade of work signifies a fully completed project, with penmanship, neatness, accuracy, etc. of high standard. b. B grade of work signifies the completion of at least fourfifths of a project, with fair standards of penmanship, neatness, accuracy, etc. c. C grade of work signifies the completion of at least twothirds of a project, with reasonably acceptable standards of penmanship, neatness, accuracy, etc.

B. FORMAL: 

1. A vocabulary test may be given, using the word list for Unit 1, which will be found in Unit 29, page 573. 

2. Objective Test No. 1, obtainable from the publishers, may be given.

Answers to Test, Research, and Discussion Questions for Unit 1 

1. "Business is the activity that makes more and more want-satisfying goods and helpful services available to more and more people." (See text, page 1.) 

2. This is a discussion question, and student responses will vary. 

3. See "The Door Is Open," page 4; also, the chart facing page 3. 

4. Another discussion question. Student responses will vary. However, most students will mention grocery, meat, dry goods, hardware, auto supply, drug, and similar retail stores. 

5. Another discussion question. Such things as transportation, communication, electricity, gas, etc. are usually supplied by concerns not located in the student's immediate business community.

I. AIMS: 

UNIT 2-THE FIFTH CLASS PERIOD  

To help students appreciate the advantages of understanding the business community and of preparing to live in it successfully. (See "Objective," Unit 2, Table of Contents, page ix.)

B. SPECIFIC AIM: 

To show students that they must make their own decisions.

C. PROJECT AIMS (see Titles of Projects): 

1. No. 2. Educational and vocational guidance.

3. No. 4. Vocational guidance. 

II. MATERIALS: 

1. The text and project pad. 

2. A pamphlet or leaflet describing courses of study in the local school, and a college catalogue. 

Note: The school library or office usually has a number of college catalogues on file. 

B. FOR THE STUDENTS: 

The text and project pad.

III. PREPARATION AND APPROACH (Teacher Activities): 

1. By questions and rapid oral drill, review Unit 1. 

2. Emphasize the fact that a knowledge of the community (Unit 1) is essential to an intelligent choice of life work. 

Knowledge of the business community is necessary in choosing a life work because: a. Everyone lives in it. b. Everyone buys and uses the goods and services it supplies. c. Everyone earns, spends, and saves money in it. d. Everyone needs to choose and prepare for the kind of work in which he can best succeed and which he will do as a member of the business community. 

C. PRETEST: 

Ask questions 1 and 3, page 21.

A. TRANSITION: 

1. Use first two paragraphs, page 11. 

2. Write on the board, "Happiness and success come largely to those who have chosen the work best suited to them." Show how this logically follows III-A and B above. 

3. Call attention to the illustration on page 12 of the text and have some student read the legend. 

4. Develop the idea that Edison is as much of an adventurer as 

Rear Admiral Byrd. Use other examples. 

5. Call on students to mention several specific occupations in the community and list these occupations on the blackboard. 

6. Ask how long it takes and how much it costs to learn to be a lawyer, a teacher, a doctor, an accountant, a trained nurse, a stenographer, etc.

7. Have students look at the sheet for Project No. 2 and show that some decisions and choices must be made in order to work out this project. 

1. Read the directions for Project No. 2. 

2. Call attention to the fact that it costs from $60 to $200 per year to educate each public school student in the United States. If possible, find the annual per capita cost of providing educational opportunities for secondary school students in your city. Remind students that procuring and spending funds for education involve many business activities and that it is good business practice on the part of every individual to use educational opportunities to the best advantage, i.e., in a businesslike way. 

3. Open a college or private school catalogue to pages showing entrance requirements, length of course, tuition, and other items of cost. Read some of the items comprising the cost of college courses and tie up this information with the presentation of Project No. 2. For this purpose, the teacher might well use his own experience in meeting the expenses of attending college. 

4. Word study for Unit 2 (see Unit 29): 

1. Students' summaries of previous lesson. (See III-A-1 above.) 2. Students' discussion of III-B above. 

3. Students' answers to questions 1 and 3, page 21. (See III-C above.) 

4. Students' discussion of the personality and character traits, activities, and achievements of the men represented in the picture on page 12 and of such parallel examples as Edison and 

Byrd. (See IV-A-3, 4 above.) 

5. Students' responses to IV-A-5 above. 

6. Students' answers to questions suggested in IV-A-6 above. 

7. Reading by students of directions for Project No. 2, page 22, of the text, and the column headings, etc. on the project form. 

8. Students' pronunciation, spelling, definition, and use of words included in vocabulary study for this unit. (See IV-B-4 above.) 

Note: Pronunciation, spelling, and correct use are more important than the ability to give suitable dictionary definitions.

After each objective test has been scored, test sheets should be returned to students and a tabulation of the answers should be prepared. For this purpose, the teacher may write the question numbers on the blackboard and ask all students who missed the first question to raise their hands. Proceed in like manner with other questions. In this way the teacher will be able to determine which questions have been most difficult for students. The information revealed in this way should be used as the basis of review and remedial teaching.

I. AIMS: 

UNIT 3-THE EIGHTH CLASS PERIOD  

To develop those attitudes and appreciations that seem to be necessary in order to live in any business community. (See 

"Objective," Unit 3, Table of Contents, page ix.)  

1. To help students appreciate the fact that education is the first necessary step in a businesslike preparation for life work. 

2. To help students understand how education helps to meet the demand of modern business. 

3. To help students realize that business principles and practices are in accord with the Seven Cardinal Objectives of Education. 

C. PROJECT AIMS (see Project Titles): 

1. No. 5. Self-direction and self-guidance. 

2. No. 6. Health guidance. 

3. No. 7. Educational and vocational guidance. 

II. MATERIALS: 

1. The text and project pad. 

2. A leaflet, booklet, or chart describing a health program. 

3. Library books on biography, choosing careers, occupations, achieving success, etc.

B. FOR THE STUDENTS: 

1. The text and project pad. 

2. A card or schedule showing the student's daily program of subjects and other school activities.

III. PREPARATION AND APPROACH (Teacher Activities): 

Emphasize the fact that Projects Nos. 1, 2, 3, and 4 form a sound basis for making decisions. "Now we are ready to use those decisions as a guide for a businesslike preparation for life work." (This is also a part of the transition process.) 

Call on several students to give the reasons why each is in school.

C. PRETEST: 

Such questions as the teacher may ask on the assignment for today's lesson.

A. TRANSITION: 

1. Use first paragraph, page 23. 

2. Initiate a brief discussion of the fact that everyone must choose a life work before he can prepare for it intelligently. 

3. Have some student read the Seven Cardinal Objectives of

Education and emphasize the fact that these objectives are the very foundation of all American education, and that they are not only good education but also "good business" for the individual. 

4. Devote a few minutes to a consideration of the course of study for your own school and say, "It required several thousand years of civilization to build this course of study. Therefore, it is worthy of serious consideration and great respect." Then draw a line on the blackboard several feet long and letter certain sections as follows: 

C D E F Then explain that this line represents the estimated period of time during which man has lived upon the earth-some 

200,000 years. A represents the beginning of man's existence;

B represents about 4000 B.C., or the beginning of known history; C represents the year 1 A.D.; D represents the discovery of America; E represents the beginning of public education in New England as early as 1647; and F represents the present time. progress 

It is the

The line 

The line B-F represents the period during which the of mankind can be more or less accurately traced. record of civilization and is not a very long time. 

E-F represents the period of public education—a very precious thing, to be guarded, cultivated, and preserved. Then draw another line and divide it into sections as follows: A B C D This second line, in comparison with the first, represents the progress that has been made in public education during a period of less than 300 years as compared with the progress made since the beginning of mankind. Point to the rapid increase in the comforts, conveniences, and luxuries that have made life more and more pleasant for people since the beginning of public education, and especially during the last 100 years.

Point out that there must be some relation between the progress in education and the progress in business, because business is the great social agency that provides more and more of the things that more and more people need and want as civilization progresses. Courses of study like the one being followed have not been in existence for a very long time, and they are being changed and improved from year to year to meet new needs. 

5. Turn to page 18 of the text and ask students if Egyptian, Roman, or early Chinese boys and girls could have used such information as an aid in deciding what kind of work they would do. 

1. Have students turn to the form for Project No. 5, and after a brief discussion of the information in Sections 1 and 2, which should have been completed at this time, suggest that they do their best to improve the use of their time before Section 3 of the same project is to be completed. 

2. Present question 2, page 32, for class discussion. Have several students relate their experiences with the thrift program of the school. Make this specific if the school maintains a savings bank, and try to give pupils some actual facts about saving and withdrawing. 

3. Present question 5 on page 33 for discussion. Tie this up directly with the school and community safety and health programs. Have students bring in any leaflets, pamphlets, booklets, etc. that describe or explain such programs. 

4. Have students turn to the picture on page 27. The lesson to be drawn from this picture is as follows: In order to enjoy leisure, business men and women work, render service, earn incomes, save something, all of which enables them to build health by using leisure wisely. Business firms give employees vacations, not as a reward for faithful, efficient service, but because vacations enable employees to do better work. "All work and no play makes Jack a dull boy." 

5. Call for definitions of words and expressions, especially of those listed in the word study for this unit. (See Unit 29.) 

6. Have students read both the directions in the text (page 33) 

and the form for Project No. 6.

1. The activities suggested in IV-A above represent a combination of activities and processes, some of which might be

classified as old knowledge, some as common knowledge, some as transition, and some as new knowledge. In fact, this is true of the activities of both teacher and students in almost every well-conducted recitation. It is frequently impossible to achieve a distinct classification of activities, and there is much overlapping.

UNIT 3-THE TENTH CLASS PERIOD 

Note: This class period should so be planned as to allow twenty minutes for an objective test. (See VI-B-2 following.)

I. AIMS: 

See I, page 60, for the Eighth Class Period.

II. MATERIALS: 

The text and project pad.

B. FOR THE STUDENTS: 

The text, project pad, and notebook.

III. PREPARATION AND APPROACH (Teacher Activities): 

Brief summary of Unit 3. 

Have student turn to the chart on page 31 of the text. Have some student read it and then emphasize the fact that all students are being educated in this way-that all activities of our school have a direct bearing on each student's home, community, vocation, and leisure activities. 

A. TRANSITION: 

Discussion: People study up and work up toward success; many learn much while on the job and advance rapidly; others fail to take advantage of their opportunities, do not become more efficient, and therefore fail to advance. 

1. Call for answers to test, research, and discussion question 4.

Answers to Test, Research, and Discussion Questions for Unit 3 

1. Health and Safety; Worthy Home Membership; Mastery of the Tools, Techniques, and Spirit of Learning; Vocational and Economic 

Effectiveness; Faithful Citizenship; Wise Use of Leisure; Ethical Character.

The second and third parts of this question call for expressions of personal opinions, and answers will not be uniform.

2. Answers will depend on conditions in your own school. 

3. Honesty, courtesy, consideration for others, fair play, dependability, courage, tenacity, etc.

Answers to the second and third parts of this question will depend on personal opinions, and are intended to provoke discussion. 

The answer to the last part of this question should be "No." Interesting discussion may be stimulated by asking which qualities are most important, and then requiring each student to defend his answer. 

4. Worthy Use of Leisure. 

Answers to the second part of this question will depend on local conditions.

The total wages of employees during the time allowed for their vacations is $30,000. Develop the idea, however, that business men are willing to pay employees' wages and salaries while they are on vacation because vacations enable employees to do better work and more of it. 

Undoubtedly, this business man was justified in incurring this expense. 

5. Answers to the first three parts of this question will depend almost entirely on local conditions. 

Accident prevention measures are "good business" because: (1) they help to avoid an undesirable reputation for careless management and indifference to the welfare of customers and employees, and (2) they reduce the cost of lawsuits for damages brought by injured people. 

This answer can be amplified to almost any extent.
