UNIT 4-THE ELEVENTH CLASS PERIOD I. AIMS: 

To show that present-day civilization is the result of intelligent, businesslike cooperation. (See "Objective," Unit 2, Table of

1. To show that the social, civic, and business institutions and the people that comprise them cooperate for the mutual benefit of all citizens. 

2. To show that education, government, and business are possible only through cooperation of every individual concerned. 

C. PROJECT AIMS (see Project Titles): 

1. No. 8. To emphasize the benefits that citizens enjoy as the result of community cooperation. 

2. No. 9. To emphasize the benefits resulting from the inherent rights of private ownership. 

3. No. 10. To demonstrate further the effects and benefits of community cooperation. 

4. No. 11. To show how cooperation makes life more convenient, comfortable, and pleasant—an appreciation of business as a social agency. 

5. No. 12. To call attention to the specific business firms in the student's community that contribute to the welfare of its citizens-appreciation and attitude development. 

II. MATERIALS: 

1. The textbook and project pad. 

2. Electric light, telephone, or gas company receipt.

B. FOR THE STUDENTS: 

The text, project pad, and notebook.

III. PREPARATION AND APPROACH (Teacher Activities): 

Call attention to the fact that certain kinds of animals gather together in flocks, herds, packs, etc. for protection—for the common welfare.

People likewise gather together for a common purpose. In early times, the principal reason for this was mutual protection from common dangers. This instinct for protection (common welfare) was undoubtedly the initial motive that gave rise to community life.

In modern life, everyone enjoys many

different advantages resulting from community life, and no one can fully escape his responsibility as an individual citizen in his community. To a greater or less extent, mutual cooperation by every citizen results in mutual benefit to all citizens. 

C. PRETESTS: 

Such questions as the teacher may see fit to ask.

A. TRANSITION: 

1. Use both paragraphs, page 34. 

2. Exhibit an electric light, gas, or telephone company receipt. 

Call on students to mention some of the different types of workers in such business organizations that must cooperate in order to make electricity available to users or consumers.

Miners, lumbermen, workers of all kinds in transportation companies, engineers, surveyors, electricians, bookkeepers, stenographers, clerks, etc., all must cooperate in order to make electric service possible. 

3. On the blackboard list different types of workers that cooperate in the production and distribution of such things as cotton, sugar, wool, meat, silk, etc. 

4. Ask, What do you think would happen if all railroad employees were to stop working? Telephone company employees? 

Firemen? Policemen? Doctors? Teachers?

1. Have students turn to the picture on page 35 and name the different types of business men represented-farmer, shipper, banker, manufacturer, retailer, and the telephone operator in the background. These business men and their business activities are dependent upon one another. Contrast this interdependence with conditions that existed in the life of the cave man represented in the lower right-hand corner of the picture. 

2. Show that free public education is one of the finest examples of community cooperation. Without education, civilization would be impossible. Each person is an important and useful member of his community and must "take a willing and intelligent part in the affairs of the world" and thus help in passing the torch of civilization along to future generations, so that they can enjoy even greater advantages. 

3. Have students turn to the chart on page 39 of the text, which is the basis of another teaching situation. Ask a number of questions that may be answered from the chart, as, for example: a. Name the activities in the average community. (The items in the first column.)

b. Name the means by which production, distribution, financing, etc. are carried on. (The items in the second column.) c. Name the purposes or reasons for these activities. (The explanations in the last column.)

Note: Numerous other questions and the answers to them may be developed from this chart. 

4. Call for answers to test, research, and discussion questions 1 and 2, page 44.

Have students read the directions (text pages 44-45) and the form for Projects Nos. 8 and 9; present enough statistics of your own school system to supply the students with the necessary data in order to work out Section 1 of Project No. 8; urge students to use the telephone directory in finding the answers to some of the items in Sections 2 and 3 of this project; tie up “The Rights of Private Ownership," on page 37 of the text, with Project No. 9.

UNIT 4-THE FOURTEENTH CLASS PERIOD I. AIMS:

Have students refer to completed Project No. 11. Ask them if modern manufacturing would be possible if it were not for inventions. Call for reasons. Would inventions be of any use if it were not for manufacturing, distribution, and consumption? If it were not for invention, production or manufacturing, and distribution, how would people get the things they must have to supply their needs and wants? Would we enjoy as many comforts, conveniences, and luxuries of life as we do under present conditions? What would be the effect on presentday conditions if inventors had not discovered and learned how to use electricity?

1. See IV-A above. This is another example of overlapping of processes in the activities of the recitation. Bring out that inventions help to speed up production or manufacturing, and thus help to satisfy the needs and wants of consumers. 

2. Discuss and explain the directions, text page 45, and the blank form for Project No. 12. This is an extremely helpful and valuable project. Read or have some student read the directions on page 45 of the text very carefully and have all students turn to the blank form in the text.

Note: Obviously, not all kinds of business activity listed on the form for this project will be represented in every community. 

If is just as important to know this as it is to know the names of firms that are engaged in the various kinds of activities. 

When some particular kind of activity is not represented in your community, ask whether anything can be and is done to make up for its absence. For example, fishing and lumbering activities will not be represented in many communities. Does this mean that consumers must do without fish and lumber? 

Many similar ideas underlie this project, which is prolific in possibilities for emphasizing the social significances and values of business.

Undoubtedly. Interpreting the records of what has happened in a particular business is absolutely essential to successful business management and ownership. That is the most important reason for accounting records. The second part of this question can be elaborated upon to a considerable extent.

COMPREHENSIVE UNIT LESSON PLANS FOR UNITS 5-14 

The remaining lesson plans are based on complete units of subject matter rather than on daily class periods. Observation as well as classroom and administrative experience have long since convinced the authors that any attempt to set up in advance, for any considerable length of time, a daily schedule of progress or

achievement in the use of classroom materials is bound to break down sooner or later because of events and circumstances that cannot be foreseen and that are beyond control. Specific aims or objectives of the daily recitation must depend to a considerable extent upon what happens from day to day during the recitation and, above all, upon the judgment of the teacher. The same is true of remedial instruction and of most of the other activities and processes characterizing recitations that are in accord with modern educational practice.

Although to continue to set up lesson plans on a daily basis arbitrarily for teachers would not be practicable in a manual of this kind, the authors believe that most teachers will want to prepare their own plans for each class period. For that purpose, the following comprehensive unit plans will be found extremely helpful. In these unit plans, the general, the specific, and the project aims for each unit have been set up; primary and secondary materials for both the teacher and the student have been suggested; and each unit of subject matter has been broken up into a series of assignments that are in accord with the experimental outline on pages 17-29 of this manual. Desirable teacher activities are also suggested in Sections III and IV of each unit lesson plan, but the suggested student activities in Section V are limited both in number and in variety, because they must always depend on teacher activities.

UNIT 5-BUSINESS ETHICS AND INDIVIDUAL CHARACTER I. AIMS: 

To show that business has developed ethical standards of practice and to develop an appreciation of the ethics and traits. that are of help to every person in developing his own character. (See "Objective," Unit 5, Table of Contents, page x.)

1. To show how and why business has developed high standards. of ethical practice.

2. To develop a realization of the importance to the individual student of desirable character traits and to point out the means by which such traits may be developed. 

3. Other specific aims will depend largely on daily assignments. and on developments during recitations.

C. PROJECT AIMS (see Project Titles): 

1. No. 13. To develop an appreciation of the importance of self-study, of desirable character traits, and of self-improvement. 

2. No. 14. To show one means by which ethical standards may be raised. 

3. No. 15. To show that the ethics of modern business are of a higher standard than formerly, and to demonstrate the advantages of higher standards. 

4. No. 16. To show some of the means by which business standards have been raised and to point out one of the underlying purposes of school organizations and clubs. The latter is on the student-experience level. 

5. No. 17. Guidance and self-direction—to demonstrate that good character and reputation are essential to a successful business career.

II. MATERIALS: 

1. The text and project pad.

2. Additional material: 

a. Several professional magazines intended for teachers. b. A civics or other text or reference book that explains the functions of the Department of Commerce of the United 

States Government. 

c. Some Government pamphlet or publication. d. A code of ethics or the constitution and by-laws of some professional or business club or other organization, if possible.

B. FOR THE STUDENTS (Primary): 

1. The text, project pad, and notebook. 

2. A few good books from the school library on biography, character, careers, success, etc. (See Bibliography.)

Class library. Codes of ethics, mottoes, slogans, etc., appro priate to the subject matter of the unit; also publications of such organizations as the Chamber of Commerce of the United States, the local chamber of commerce, the Better Business 

Bureau, Rotary Club, Kiwanis Club, Lions Club, etc.

III. PREPARATION AND APPROACH (Teacher Activities): 

Daily summary and review of the preceding lesson according to the judgment and discretion of the teacher. 

Whenever possible, draw on the students' general knowledge and everyday experiences to serve as a foundation on which to build the other activities and processes of each recitation. 

The knowledge and daily activities of adolescent students are surprisingly broad and numerous, and to the resourceful teacher will prove a prolific source of materials upon which to draw.

C. PRETESTS: 

Usually, questions propounded by the teacher, but occasionally one of the test, research, and discussion questions will also serve this purpose. In this unit, question 4, page 63, may be used.

A. TRANSITION: 

1. Use first two paragraphs, page 46. 

2. Display the civics text and read, or have some student read, the description and explanation of the activities of the Department of Commerce of the United States Government. The 

Government is a familiar institution to all students, but often that familiarity is more or less vague and unorganized. Hence, this is a good way to relate common knowledge to the new topics. 

3. Display a Government pamphlet and explain the nature of its contents. This will show that the Government is trying to render service and raise standards in many fields of activity. 

4. Display a professional teachers' magazine as a means of showing how organization helps to raise standards. If possible, read some code of ethics, motto, or pledge to which teachers subscribe. 

5. Write, "Honesty is the best policy," on the blackboard and explain that to observe this proverb is "good business." 

Hence, it is worthy of serious consideration and strict observance by every person who is interested in his own welfare. 

2. Call on students for answers to test, research, and discussion questions.

4. At appropriate times during the presentation of this unit keep in mind the following suggestions: Use Project No. 15 as a teaching device. Discuss it with students and raise questions of service, fair price, accurate weights, possibility of exchange, and helpfulness that are extended to customers by wandering merchants and peddlers, etc. What kind of service do merchants render when they depend upon an ever-changing group of chance customers? Do they render better or worse service if they are permanently located in stores of their own and must depend on the return of the same general group of customers time after time?

Turn to the chart on pages 54-56. Have students read from it answers to such questions as these: Membership in the Grange is open to what group of people? What are its purpose and plan? To what organization do office managers belong?

What is its purpose? This chart provides almost unlimited opportunities for such questions. Do not overlook the importance of tying up this chart with Project No. 16. 

Call on students to enumerate the twelve desirable traits of character as given on pages 58-59 and list them on the blackboard. Make every effort to get students to define each trait in terms of action, that is, emphasize trait actions rather than abstract ideas. Example: A storekeeper is honest when he puts 16 ounces of sugar in each pound; when he makes change correctly; when he makes sure that a customer's order for a dozen oranges does not include one that is slightly spoiled; when he explains exactly and accurately what certain things are or what they will do, etc.

Ask what business men do to protect themselves against some of the risks of business. How do banks prevent losses due to burglary? Are all people honest?

Have students open their project pads to the form for No. 17. 

Have some member of the class read the details on this form aloud. Then analyze it somewhat as follows: a. The first part tells who the applicant is. b. The next part tells of his experience. c. The next part gives the applicant's sponsors or referencesthe names of people who know the applicant and, as a result of their acquaintance, can recommend him. Our character is the sum total of what we are; our reputation is what others think we are. Without a good character, a good reputation is practically impossible. It pays to have a good character.

d. It is, therefore, "good business" for students to make good with their teachers, their principal, and their associates, because sometime they may want to call upon them to vouch for them. For example, when a bonding company receives an application like the one used in this project, it writes to all the people named as references in order to verify the accuracy of the statements that have been made by the applicant. The bond is not issued until the company is sure of the applicant's character. 

5. Call for students' reports on any supplementary reading that may have been assigned.

1. Students' discussion of topics previously assigned for study. 

2. Student responses to test, research, and discussion questions and other questions propounded by the teacher. 

3. Students' discussions of completed projects. 

4. Students' responses to other activities suggested in III and 

IV above. 

5. Students' reports on any supplementary reading that may have been assigned.

B. ASSIGNED HOME WORK OR SUPERVISED STUDY: 

1. The following daily assignments are based on the "Experimental Outline for a Two-Semester Course," on page 19 of this manual. 

a. First day. Read and study Unit 5 to "Cooperation Elimi nates Unfair Competition," page 52; work out Project

No. 15. 

b. Second day. Read and study text from "Cooperation 

Eliminates Unfair Competition," page 52, to bottom of page 56. Prepare to answer test, research, and discussion questions 1 and 2, page 62; work out Project No. 16. c. Third day. Prepare to answer test, research, and discussion questions 3 and 4, pages 62-63; work out Project No. 14. d. Fourth day. Prepare to answer test, research, and discussion question 5, page 63; work out Projects Nos. 13 and 17. (Assignment limited in order to allow for Ob jective Test No. 5.)  

3. Materials for daily class period: the text, notebook, project pad, and such other materials as the teacher may direct. 

None suggested for this unit.

VI. TESTING: 

A. INFORMAL: 

1. Daily questions, etc. 

2. Inspection of projects: 

a. Project No. 13 is not to be handed in or graded. b. When completed, Project No. 14 may be read by two or three different students who have procured different codes. c. If possible, answers to completed Project No. 15 should be read in class, because they provide excellent discussion material. d. Project No. 16 is based on the chart, pages 54-56. More names of local organizations may be found in a telephone directory and city directory. A very complete list may be compiled as a composite of all completed projects. e. UNIT 6-THE LEVELS OF OCCUPATION AND RESPONSIBILITY I. AIMS: 

To understand the levels of occupation and responsibility in the various fields of business activity. (See "Objective,"

Unit 6, Table of Contents, page x.)  

1. To show that there are three levels of occupation, reponsibility, opportunity, reward, and education. 

2. To show that each of these levels exists in the nine great fields of business activity. 

3. To show that education hastens advancement and multiplies opportunities. 

4. Other specific aims will depend largely on daily assignments and developments during recitations.

C. PROJECT AIMS (see Project Titles): 

1. No. 18. To show: (a) that people who have college educations are more apt to achieve prominence than those who do not, and (b) that education and attendance at school are not one and the same thing.

Daily summary and review of the preceding lesson according to the judgment and discretion of the teacher.

1. Draw on students' general knowledge and everyday experiences. 

2. Call on students to enumerate their occupational experiences. 

C. PRETEST: 

By questions. Use 3, pages 86-87.

A. TRANSITION: 

1. Use first two paragraphs, page 67. 

2. Use Project No. 18 as the basis of the transition process in at least one recitation. In subsequent lessons on this unit, the other projects may be made to serve a similar purpose. 

3. Exhibit the bookkeeping, shorthand, and college texts, and explain that knowledge and training acquired from courses in these subjects enable young people to obtain positions and to win promotion. Explain, for example, how a high school. graduate who, by means of courses in shorthand and typewriting, has acquired stenographic skill, can probably obtain a position on the senior level of employment with excellent chances for promotion to the leadership level without spending

any, or at least very much, time on the junior level. Explain further that knowledge and training obtained in courses of college grade sometimes enable people to start work immediately in some of the learned professions, which are on the leadership level, without spending any, or at least very much, time on either the junior or the senior levels. In other words, knowledge and training can frequently be substituted for a considerable amount of experience. 

4. List on the blackboard a few occupations on the junior level and emphasize the limited reward and opportunities for advancement. Then list a number of occupations on the senior level for which a high school course is adequate preparation and point to the greater rewards and better opportunities for advancement.

2. Call on students for answers to test, research, and discussion questions. 

4. Have students turn to the chart on page 71 and read the three legends in the bottom section. Using the chart as the basis for further study of all levels of occupation and education, ask what kinds of jobs are open to a boy or a girl with only a junior high school education; to young people with a high school education; to people with college training. Observe that, in this chart, responsibility and supervision are shown by the words "Great," "Considerable," and "Little" in inverse ratio. 

5. Use the chart, "Education Pays," on pages 84-85 as the basis of a student discussion in order to visualize the dollar-andcents value of education. This chart and the one mentioned above enable students to see that "education hastens advancement and multiplies opportunities." These charts are prolific in opportunities for resultful discussion. 

6. Ask numerous questions about the three levels of occupation, pages 67-70, as How can a worker on the lowest level advance to the senior level? Is it impossible for him to advance to the leadership level? Do many actually achieve advancement to the highest level? 

7. Emphasize the education that characterizes most workers on the three levels of occupation. In times of widespread unemployment, which group of people have the hardest time to hold their jobs or to obtain new jobs? Compare the earning power of workers on the various levels of occupation. Does

education alone qualify people for occupations on the leadership level? 

8. Have students turn to Project No. 18. Mention several 

Americans who had limited schooling (but considerable selfeducation), such as Lincoln, Burbank, Edison, Ford, etc.

Famous Americans who enjoyed the benefits of a college education are: Roosevelt, Woodrow Wilson, Taft, Coolidge, Hoover, Byrd, and many others. Do not fail to point out that many of these well-known Americans who did not attend college, or even high school, were really well-educated people. 

9. In preparing Projects Nos. 19 and 20, it will be well to suggest that the names of the people interviewed need not necessarily be given. Students may interview members of their families, intimate friends, etc., for this information.

Nos. 2 and 4 respectively. As usual, the directions and forms should be read aloud in class prior to their assignment for preparation as home work or supervised study. These two projects should be presented as business documents-business men plan for the future every day and it is "good business" for the student to do likewise. Write on the blackboard: "Business men plan their futures; we can use businesslike methods to good advantage in planning our own futures."

UNIT 7-PROBLEMS OF FINDING EMPLOYMENT AND BUSINESS OWNERSHIP I. AIMS: 

To develop an understanding and appreciation of the problems and opportunities of working for someone else and of business ownership. (See "Objective," Unit 7, Table of Contents, page x.)

1. To emphasize the point of view of the employer, what he demands in employees, and how he tries to select them. 

2. To emphasize the employer's point of view as an aid to those who seek employment. 

3. To show how to use intelligent and efficient means of finding employment.

Note: This is done early in the course to avoid giving the impression that the completion of this course in business science qualifies students for employment, thereby encouraging them to leave school instead of remaining until their education, training, and maturity are acceptable to employers. 

4. To present briefly a few of the elementary problems that confront the business owner. 

5. Other specific aims are dependent largely on daily assignments and developments during recitations.

C. PROJECT AIMS (see Project Titles): 

1. No. 26. Vocational guidance. 

2. No. 27. Self-study and direction.

Note: Only columns numbered 1 should be filled in at this time, and the completed project should not be discussed in class for obvious reasons.

3. No. 28. Vocational guidance, with localized community application emphasized. 

4. No. 29. Guidance and developing judgment. Observe the three-level attainment possibilities of this project. 

5. No. 30. Educational and vocational guidance; also character trait development. 

6. No. 31. Vocational guidance; letter-writing practice. 

7. No. 32. Educational and vocational guidance; also character development.

Note: Observe that filling in all these project forms is also precise clerical practice.

1. The text, project pad, and manual.

The first section of this project tells who you are. b. The second part tells what your education and training are. c. The last question on the form asks you to state specifically what you can do.

UNIT 8-MODERN BUSINESS ORGANIZATION AND OWNERSHIP I. AIMS: 

To develop a recognition and appreciation of the three types of present-day business organizations. (See "Objective,"

Unit 8, Table of Contents, page xi.)  

1. To show the nature, advantages, disadvantages, and limitations of the individual-proprietorship type of business organization.

2. To show the nature, advantages, disadvantages, and limitations of the partnership type of business organization. 

3. Other specific aims are dependent largely on daily assignments and developments during recitations.

C. PROJECT AIM: 

There are no projects for use with this unit.

II. MATERIALS: 

The text and project pad.

B. FOR THE STUDENTS (Primary): 

1. The text, project pad, and notebook. 

2. Any newspaper or magazine articles or illustrations pertinent to types of business organization. 

Note: Clippings, pictures, advertisements, etc. that students voluntarily collect help to measure their interest and ought to be considered as extra-credit material. 

Left to the teacher's discretion.

III. PREPARATION AND APPROACH (Teacher Activities): 

Daily summary and review of the preceding lesson according to the judgment and discretion of the teacher. 

1. Call on students for the names of local stores and other business enterprises with which they are familiar. 

Note: Some of the responses to this suggestion will undoubtedly include chain stores operated by large corporations, while others will include news stands, drug stores, meat stores, etc. that represent single proprietorships. Call attention to the two different types of ownership. 

2. Have students refer to completed Project No. 12 in their notebooks and call on different students to name those firms that they know represent single proprietorships, partnerships, and corporations. 

Note: The partnership is a comparatively rare type of organization. Undoubtedly, most of the firms listed in Project 

No. 12 will represent either the individual proprietorship or the corporation type of organization. 

3. The resourceful teacher will see many other opportunities in this and the following unit to draw on students' general knowledge and everyday experiences for a foundation on which to build new knowledge.

C. 

PRETEST: 

Usually, questions propounded by the teacher, but occasionally one or more of the test, research, and discussion questions will also serve this purpose if asked in advance of preparation. 

A. TRANSITION: 

1. See the opening paragraph, page 113 of this unit. 

2. The resourceful teacher will undoubtedly find many opportunities for amplifying the transition processes in her own and in the students' experiences, as well as in the developments in the daily recitation. 

2. Call on students for answers to test, research, and discussion questions. 

3. At appropriate times during the presentation of this unit, keep in mind the following suggestions:

Call on students to name several kinds of businesses that could not very well be owned and operated either as a single proprietorship or as a partnership. Why? 

Ask such questions as these: What is a partnership?

How is a partnership formed? What are articles of co partnership? Give some reasons why a man who owned a small factory might take two other men into partnership. 

Have some student read aloud the articles of copartnership on pages 119-121 of the text and discuss the terms very briefly. The principal purpose of presenting the articles of copartnership at this time is that it serves as an excellent means of emphasizing the need for exercising considerable care before entering into any agreement and, especially a partnership with others. It is not intended that students should comprehend the legal implications of such documents. 

Use the chart on page 122 as the basis of a teaching situation. Call attention to the divided responsibilities of the owners of this factory and point out that each of the three partners is apparently more or less of a specialist. Compare the chart with the one on page 116 and point out the fundamental differences of the two types of business ownership. 

The only fundamental difference between the individual proprietorship and the partnership lies in the divided ownership of the latter. If the factory represented in the chart on page 122 were owned by a single proprietor, he would undoubtedly find it advisable to hire two or three capable

employees, each of whom would supervise certain groups of the activities.

The vast number of small retail stores and shops of almost every variety shows the great importance of the individual proprietorship, especially in the field of distribution, which lies between the producer and the consumer. 

Point out, however, that as retail businesses increase in size, there is a greater tendency toward the partnership, but more especially toward the corporation type of ownership. 

4. Call on students for definitions of words and expressions. 

5. Call on students for reports of any supplementary reading that may have been assigned. 

1. Students' discussion of topics previously assigned for study. 

2. Student responses to the activities suggested in III and IV above. 

3. Students' reports on any supplementary reading that may have been assigned for this unit.

UNIT 9-MODERN I. AIMS: 

BUSINESS ORGANIZATION AND OWNERSHIP (Continued) 

See Aims for Unit 8.

1. To show the nature, advantages, disadvantages, and limitations of the corporation type of business organization. 

2. Other specific aims will depend largely on daily assignments and developments during recitations.

C. PROJECT AIMS (see Project Titles): 

1. Nos. 33, 34, and 35-important. It is not intended that the documents that serve as the forms for these three projects should be presented to students as papers for technical study and understanding. It is enough if they know that such documents are used by and are peculiar to the corporate type of business ownership, and what general purpose each document serves.

These three projects afford precise practice in filling out business forms and in following instructions. This is clerical training of the most effective kind. 

2. No. 36. To apply the textual explanation of the three types of business organization on the basis of the students' community experiences and contacts.

A. TRANSITION: 

1. Use the first two paragraphs of page 124 for this purpose. 

2. Have students compare the chart on page 135 with those on pages 116 and 122. Point out that the titles of the owners and of the executive and administrative officers of the corporation represented by the chart on page 135 constitute the only fundamental difference between the corporation type of organization and the individual proprietorship or the partnership. All the activities over which the general manager of the corporation has supervision would not be any different if this knitting mill were owned and operated as a single proprietorship or as a partnership. 

3. The directions in the text and the blank form in the pad for 

Project No. 36 can also be used to good advantage in the transition process. In this way, the students' knowledge of their community can be tied up with the study of Units 8 and 9.

4. Call on students for definitions of words and phrases. 

5. At appropriate times, call on students to answer such questions as these: What is a corporation? How is a corporation formed?

By what authority? Do the actual owners of a very large corporation having many stockholders actually manage its affairs? What are the advantages, disadvantages, and limitations of the corporation, especially as compared with the individual proprietorship and partnership?

Have students mention names of local corporations if there are any that represent examples of business enterprises mentioned on pages 117-118 of the text. 

The directions in the text and the form for Project No. 33 may be used as a test of the students' ability to follow directions. Junior typists in many business offices are required to fill in the blank spaces in articles of incorporation, stock certificates, stock transfer certificates, insurance policies, contracts, and many other kinds of legal documents. To do such work, they must be able to follow directions. On the other hand, they are not expected to understand the impli-

cations of such documents or even to read much of the printed matter on them. Such forms as the one used for this project are excellent materials for clerical practice, but the legal implications should not be emphasized. 

The stock certificates used in Projects Nos. 34 and 35 are intended to help students recognize them as special corporation forms and to understand their purpose as evidence of ownership of capital stock. Develop the following points in connection with these certificates: a. They are documents certifying ownership of capital stock. b. They do not promise any dividends, profits, or even the return of the original cost.

Project No. 36 affords students excellent practice in the use of their powers of observation and in learning something more about the business concerns in their community. It also affords practice in the use of the telephone and city directories, which are reference books. 

Note: In connection with such projects, it is well to encourage students to get local information, but it should be done in such a way that business people will not be annoyed. For example, in Project No. 36 a casual question asked of a clerk or the manager of a local store is all that is necessary to obtain the desired information.

The individual proprietorship existed before the partnership and long before the corporation. 

2. Undoubtedly, the corporation type of organization is best because of the reasons enumerated on page 134, especially Nos. 1, 4, and 6.

A railroad, a bank, a telephone company, a telegraph company, a large automobile factory are examples of responses that may be expected to the second part of this question.

The single-proprietorship type of organization is obviously best for a news stand, but the tea room described would apparently be owned and operated as a partnership. The chain store would be operated by a corporation. The restaurant described is obviously a partnership. The gasoline station and refreshment stand is a single proprietorship. The railroad is obviously a corporation.

The dividend is a distribution of profits of a corporation among its stockholders in proportion to the number of shares each stockholder owns. 

A stockholder is an owner of capital stock in a corporation. 

Common stock and corporation stock.

A person would have to own 2,001 shares in order to control the affairs of this corporation to suit himself.

No, $25 would not necessarily be the price at which a share of stock in this corporation could be bought. In fact, after the corporation is organized and has been in operation for even a short time, it is altogether probable that the stock would not sell at exactly $25 per share.

No, not necessarily, although most very large businesses are owned and operated as corporations.

UNIT 10-COOPERATION BETWEEN GOVERNMENT AND BUSINESS I. AIMS: 

To develop an understanding and appreciation of how stable government cooperates with business for the good of all citizens. (See "Objective," Unit 10, Table of Contents, page xi.)

1. To show how organized government protects, fosters, and encourages business. 

2. To show that cooperation between government and business is essential to the welfare and prosperity of citizens. 

3. To point out that our Federal government engages in extensive business activities and actually operates a limited number of business enterprises. 

4. Other specific aims will depend largely on daily assignments and developments during recitations.

C. PROJECT AIMS (see Project Titles): 

1. No. 37. To emphasize the Declaration of Independence as a business document. 

2. No. 38. To emphasize the Constitution of the United States as a business document. 

3. No. 39. To give a concrete example of how government fosters, protects, and encourages both individual achievement and certain kinds of business activities. 

4. No. 40. To use a localized community study as a means of developing an appreciation of government.

5. No. 41. To show that many different kinds of workers and of business enterprises are subject to more or less regulation and supervision by government.

II. MATERIALS: 

1. The text, project pad, and manual.

2. Additional materials: a copy of the Declaration of Inde pendence and a copy of the Constitution of the United States; also a copy of your state constitution, if possible. 

B. FOR THE STUDENTS (Primary): 

1. The textbook, project pad, and notebook.

2. Other materials: copies of the Declaration of Independence, and of the Constitution of the United States. (Usually to be found in textbooks on history, civics, etc.) 

1. Class library. One copy of the Constitution and of the 

Declaration of Independence ought to be filed permanently as a part of the class library. 

2. Class exhibit and bulletin board. When completed, the best copy of Project No. 37 and the best copy of No. 38 might be posted on the bulletin board. In a unique way, these two projects show the interdependence of government and business as social institutions.

III. PREPARATION AND APPROACH (Teacher Activities): 

Daily summary and review of the preceding lesson according to the judgment and discretion of the teacher.

1. Call on several students to mention things that national, state, and city governments do. 

2. Ask whether policemen, letter carriers, and firemen are employees of national, state, or city governments. 

3. Ask what each of these government workers does to cooperate with business.

It is in the nature of an application for a permit to organize a corporation under the laws of the state of Ohio. 

It will give pupils an appreciation of some of the government regulations that must be complied with in order to organize a corporation.

Extensive business activities carried on in every part of the world require protection by extensive government activities.

States helps business. (See page 151.)

Answers to Test, Research, and Discussion Questions for Unit 10 1. England's ability to produce a surplus of manufactured products and to distribute them to other sections of the world in exchange for needed food and raw materials. 

There are several reasons why England has maintained a powerful navy, but, for the purpose of the second part of this question, the protection of shipping is the reason that should be sought from students. 

Business prospered in England because it was fostered and protected by a stable, sympathetic, and unified government. Business declined in 

Italy because of the lack of these things. 

2. Besides a plentiful supply of foodstuffs, a great variety of raw materials, and a large population, a country must have a stable government in order to prosper. There must also be intelligent management of business and a constantly increasing supply of capital with which to

finance the production, manufacturing, and distribution of goods. (See pages 146-148.) In the beginning, the United States undoubtedly began and continued to grow because of the immense supply and great variety of raw materials. 

3. Capital comes from savings. Individuals accumulate capital by saving a part of their wages, salaries, or other income. Business enterprises acquire capital (a) from their owners in the form of investments, (b) by borrowing from others, and (c) by retaining some of their profits. "Profit is the money you don't waste in running your business."

It is unlikely that any business enterprise would continue to grow larger and more profitable if the owner withdrew all the profits each year. 

Obviously, unstable government prevents business prosperity. Therefore, countries beset by frequent revolutions are not prosperous and their citizens cannot live as comfortably and conveniently as we do in the 

United States. 

4. By granting land to railroad companies. Later, the railroads sold most of the land and used the capital received in that way for construction purposes. The United States Government operates the Alaska Railroad and the Panama Canal. There are other examples, also. 

As a general policy, the United States Government does not own and operate business enterprises. 

5. Railroads, telegraph companies, telephone companies, street-car companies, banks, insurance companies, are examples of business enterprises that are supervised and regulated, but not owned, by national or state governments.

Such supervision seem necessary in order to protect the interests of citizens, to insure adequate service at reasonable cost, and to promote the general welfare. (See pages 153-159.)

Responses to the fourth part of this question will vary. However, there are very few kinds of business enterprises that are not supervised, to some extent at least, although the supervision may be indirect. As students give individual answers, the teacher will generally be able to cite some example of either direct or indirect supervision, inspection, or control.

There is a very great difference between government ownership and government supervision or control of business. Although the answer to this question is a simple "Yes," it is prolific in its opportunities for discussion.

2. Marriott, C., "Uncle Sam's Business," Harper & Brothers.

1. Sparks, F. M., "Business of Government," Rand McNally & Company. 

2. Cooke, M. L., "Public Utility Regulation," The Ronald Press Company.

UNIT 11-WORKING FOR THE GOVERNMENT I. AIMS: 

To introduce the student to the duties and responsibilities of government work, with special emphasis on civil-service employment and job specifications of various clerical occupations. (See "Objective," Unit 11, Table of Contents, page xi.)

1. To show that national, state, and city governments have set up job specifications for various kinds of clerical occupations. 

2. To show that there are definite duties, typical tasks, and minimum qualifications for the various kinds of government employment. 

3. To show that the standardization of jobs in civil service is also characteristic of the practice in many business offices in standardizing employment and in setting up specific job specifications. 

4. Other specific aims will depend largely on daily assignments. and developments during recitations.

4. At appropriate times during the presentation of this unit, keep in mind the following suggestions:

During the first class period devoted to this unit, discuss with students the directions in the text and the form in the pad for Project No. 43. Instruct students to have this project completed on the seventh day following this. (See V-B-1-h following.) In doing so, have students refer to completed 

Projects Nos. 26 and 28, which are of the same general type. 

Have them read the directions for No. 42 and point out what a privilege it is to have an opportunity to make such investigations.

Caution students to refrain from annoying business people. 

Many teachers profitably devote a good part of one class period to an actual drill on interviewing business people in preparation for this project. In such cases, the teacher represents the person to be interviewed and several students actually go through the process of the interview, while others comment and criticize. Other teachers appoint a committee of three or five students, each to make a separate investigation and report to the class for the benefit of other students. 

This is in accordance with the suggestion for rewarding superior attainment, pages 7-9 of this manual. 

Have students discuss working for the government as an elective or appointive officer (page 166). Are such government workers sure of permanent employment at such work? Why?

In one sense, the Federal government is a great business enterprise. It must obtain an income from various sources, which it must expend for many things, and it is the largest employer of workers in the world. In another sense, though, there is a vital difference between a government enterprise and a business enterprise. What is it? (The ultimate purpose of a business enterprise is to earn a profit; the government is not interested in profits, but merely in enough income to pay its expenses.) Have students discuss this point at length. 

If possible, display an announcement of a civil-service examination and have some student read all or at least appropriate parts, of it. Then call for discussions of "Working for the Government as a Civil-Service Employee," page 167.

Ask such questions as these: Why do we have civil service? 

What are the objectives; advantages; disadvantages? What is a civil-service commission supposed to do? (See pages 170172.)

Call attention to the fact that "job specifications" are simply lists of duties, typical tasks, and minimum qualifications for specific jobs. Civil-service commissions are constantly striving to standardize government work in this way, and many business concerns are following the same plan. 

Have sudents read the various job specifications, pages 175182. They are excellent materials for use in vocational and educational guidance. Many questions can be built upon them.

Preparatory to its assignment as home work, discuss Project No. 42 and show that the answers to each of these questions may be obtained from the job specifications, pages 175-182. Have some student read the directions in the text and have another student read the qualifications. Then ask two or three questions in order to start the students correctly in working out the project.

Answers to Test, Research, and Discussion Questions for Unit 11 

1. As a citizen, as an elective or appointive officer, and as a civil-service employee.

City mayors and state governors are chosen by popular election, i.e., by the votes of citizens.

Most students will probably say that the President of the United States is chosen by popular election, but this is not the case. He is actually chosen by the Electoral College. It might be well to have students consult an authoritative reference book. A mail carrier is a civil-service employee. 

2. This is a local question. Answers to the first part will vary, but may include such officials as the mayor and members of the city council, certain kinds of judges, and the county officers, such as county clerk, recording clerk, county treasurer, sheriff, etc. The length of their terms of office varies in different localities. 

Answers to the last part of this question will vary. Postmasters are either appointed by the President of the United States or come under the civil-service classification. Have students find out which condition applies to the local postmaster. 

3. This question calls for expressions of personal opinions, but the answer to the first part should certainly be "No." The reasons given by individual students will also vary, but see page 168. 

4. See page 169, also pages 171-172.

The advantages and disadvantages of government employment are given on pages 172-173. 

5. The three levels of responsibility and work in government employment correspond to the three levels in any other kind of occupation, namely, the junior, the senior, and the leadership level. They are exemplified in the set-up of duties, typical tasks, and minimum qualifications of the junior stenographer, the senior stenographer, and the secretarial stenographer, pages 179-180. Undoubtedly, the best way of preparing for civil-service employment is to secure at least a high school education or its equivalent (see pages 183-184).

No, the duties of the government workers mentioned in the third part of this question are not likely to differ much from the duties of the same kind of workers in the business office. 

For the educational qualifications of the junior, senior, and secretarial stenographer, see pages 179-181.

The secretarial stenographer evidently needs more education, training, and experience because the responsibilities are greater and the tasks more important than those of the junior or senior stenographer.

Crowell Company. 

2. Cooper, E. H., "How to Prepare for Civil Service," The Gregg Publishing Company. 

Young, J. T., "The New American Government and Its Work,” The Macmillan Company. 

I. AIMS: 

UNIT 12-BUSINESS LAW  

To develop an understanding and appreciation of the importance of business law. (See "Objective," Unit 12, Table of

Contents, page xi.)  

1. To show that business activities are controlled and governed by law. 

2. To develop a realization of the importance to the individual of securing competent, expert advice in certain business situations that are of common occurrence in the lives of most individuals. 

3. To illustrate the purpose and function of certain legal documents that are frequently used in the business affairs of individuals. 

4. Other specific aims will depend largely on daily assignments and developments during recitations.

C. PROJECT AIMS (see Project Titles): 

1. Nos. 46-54 inclusive. To give clerical practice in following directions and filling out forms, and to teach the purpose and function of certain legal documents that are commonly used in connection with certain typical business activities of practically all individuals. 

2. No. 55. Educational guidance and self-study (continuation of No. 23).

II. MATERIALS: 

1. The text, project pad, and manual. 

2. Additional material: commercial law textbook and, if possible, at least one volume of state laws, such as those governing

corporations or public schools; also copies of local blank apartment lease forms and other similar documents. 

B. FOR THE STUDENTS (Primary): 

1. The textbook, project pad, and notebook. 

2. Other material: blank legal forms, especially those having local significance.

1. Class library. A collection of blank legal documents, such as those suggested in B-2 above. 

2. Class exhibit. Copies of the legal forms suggested above can be used for this purpose.

III. PREPARATION AND APPROACH (Teacher Activities): 

Review and summary of preceding lesson; also of the entire unit preparatory to giving Objective Test No. 12.

1. Call on students to cite instances out of their own knowledge or experience that will illustrate the application of law to the common, everyday affairs of business enterprises and of individuals (apartment leases, insurance policies, deeds, mortgages, etc.; also inspection of food products, of weights and measures, etc.). 

2. This unit should be taught from the point of view that there are laws governing the business affairs not only of business enterprises, but also of individuals regardless of what their occupations may be. It should be used for guidance rather than for the development of technical legal knowledge. 

C. PRETESTS: 

1. Questions by the teacher. 

2. The second part of test, research, and discussion question 1 and the last part of 3, page 201, may be used for this purpose. 

3. Formal pretests may be prepared if the teacher has the time and inclination.

A. TRANSITION: 

1. Use the first two paragraphs, page 188, for this purpose.

As a matter of fact, most of the subject matter on pages 188192, inclusive, is of a transitional nature. 

2. Exhibit the commercial law text and explain that it is one of the many thousands of books on law that have grown out of an attempt to interpret the Constitution and laws of the United States and those of the several states in terms that everybody

can understand. This will help to develop an appreciation of the importance to every individual of a knowledge of the laws and regulations governing business affairs; it will also help to show the importance of taking a regular course in commercial law. 

3. Refer briefly to articles of copartnership, pages 119-120, and to the certificate of incorporation, pages 126-127, as examples of how business affairs are governed by laws. Use completed 

Project No. 33 for the same purpose. Nos. 38 and 39 may also be used.

Note: These projects should be used as practical, everyday illustrations of the constant need for some legal knowledge by everybody, but they do not need to be reviewed per se. 

2. Call on students for prepared answers to test, research, and discussion questions as assigned. 

4. At appropriate times during the presentation of this unit, keep in mind the following suggestions:

Have students refer to the job specifications on pages 175182 and pick out specific names of legal forms and documents, such as licenses, permits, bonds, coupons, purchase orders, invoices, requisitions, etc. Note especially the duties and tasks of the junior and senior clerk, pages 175-177. 

Ask such questions as these: What is private property; personal property; real property? In a general way, what was the source of English law (page 191)? What was the Law 

Merchant (page 191)? Who were Hammurabi and Justinian? (Have students look up these names in an encyclopedia.) 

Do not overlook the fact that this unit is not intended to develop technical legal knowledge. Better results will be obtained if students are not required to memorize legal definitions.

The project forms should be presented merely as examples of legal documents that are frequently used in connection with the business affairs of individuals. If students get from their study of this unit and the accompanying projects: (a) the realization that the affairs of individuals and business enterprises are controlled and governed by laws, and (b) a knowledge of the purpose and function of the documents used in the projects, the unit will have accomplished its intended purpose.

Answers to Test, Research, and Discussion Questions for Unit 12 1. All laws emanate from either one or the other of two sources: One source might be briefly described as the well-established customs and habits of people; the other source is the constitution or other document setting forth in writing the fundamental or basic laws upon which other laws are based and to which they must conform. Examples of the latter source are the Constitution of the United States and of the various states.

We must have laws to protect and enforce the rights of individuals and to have some well-understood and definitely prescribed rules of conduct that bind people or nations in their relations with each other. 

People first began to realize the need of laws when they began to live in communities.

2. Property is anything of value that may be owned. 

Personal property is generally understood to be the kind of property that may be moved about. Real property is that which is immovable, and consists of land and buildings, timber, etc., thereon. 

3. Commercial law is that body of commercial usages or rules recognized by civilized nations as relating to the rights of persons engaged in trade.

Commercial law is a required subject in so many business courses because it has to do with the common, everyday business affairs not only of business concerns, but of individuals, no matter what their occupations may be.

Everyone certainly ought to have enough knowledge or appreciation of the laws of business to be able to exercise ordinary precaution and to know when to seek expert legal advice. 

4. A contract is an agreement, based upon a valuable consideration, between two or more competent parties to do or not to do some lawful thing.

To be competent to make a binding contract, each party must be of legal age and of sufficiently sound mind and understanding to know what he is doing.

Consideration, in the legal sense, means the value that is placed on the thing that is to be done or left undone. (See page 194.)

Contracts for the sale of real estate, mortgages, and wills are examples of legal documents that must be in writing. Leases of real property and contracts involving amounts above certain stipulated sums are other examples, but local state laws stipulate conditions under which such documents must be written. 

5. A deed is an instrument in writing, under seal, by which ownership of land is transferred from one party to another. (See page 196.)

A mortgage is a conditional transfer of the title to property as security for a debt.

A lease is a written contract in which one party, called the lessor, grants permission to another party, called the lessee, to occupy or use specific real property owned by or in legal possession of the lessor. (See page 197.)

A will is a written document that provides for the distribution of a person's estate after his death.

2. Green, John B., "Law for the American Farmer," The Macmillan Company. 

3. Frederick, J. G., "The Great Game of Business," D. Appleton & Company.

1. Whigam, W. H., "Essentials of Commercial Law," The Gregg

Publishing Company. 

2. Cooley, C. H., “Human Nature and the Social Order," Charles Scribner's Sons. 

NEW FEATURES OF METHOD AND SUBJECT MATTER 

A brief consideration of the treatment of transportation in Units 13 and 14 will reveal the beginning of a new phase of both method and aims of the subject matter. Inasmuch as there is a similar treatment of the other basic divisions of business activities in succeeding units, a brief analysis of Units 13 and 14 may well be made at this time.

Unit 13 not only traces the historical development of transportation by water, land, and air, but also emphasizes its social and economic implications. The underlying purpose of the unit is to build essential knowledges, attitudes, and appreciations, and to center them on transportation as the first of the six great basic divisions of business activity, which are now to be studied from several different, but equally important, points of view. 

Unit 14 utilizes the knowledges, attitudes, and appreciations developed in Unit 13 as a foundation upon which to build guidance, exploration, and vocational practice. The student's attention is first focused on the opportunities for employment in the field of transportation. Next, he learns what kind of transportation services there are and how they may be used to the best advantage, not only by himself as an individual, but also by business organizations. Finally, he is taken around behind the counter in order to get the point of view of the distributor of transportation services and some precise practice for the twofold purpose of exploration and skill development. Certain projects localize the student's study of transportation and focus his attention on the transportation facilities in his own community and on his own daily experiences in using them.

To summarize: information, guidance, exploration, and vocational practice in properly balanced proportions are the basic elements in the treatment of transportation in these two units.

The point of view of the user or consumer of business goods and services, which has been kept in the foreground during the first twelve units of subject matter, is continued in these two and in succeeding units, but for the first time the distributor point of view is emphasized. It will be helpful to keep this analysis of the aims and method of these two units in mind as they and the succeeding units on the other basic divisions of business activity are presented.

UNIT 13-TRANSPORTATION I. AIMS: 

To understand the development of transportation and to appreciate its function in modern economic life. (See "Ob jective," Unit 13, Table of Contents.)  

1. To trace briefly the historical development of present-day transportation facilities. 

2. To help the students understand the economic importance of transportation, not only to the institution of business, but also to the individual. 

3. Other specific aims will depend largely on daily assignments and developments during recitations.

C. PROJECT AIMS (see Project Titles): 

1. No. 56. To emphasize the economic importance of transportation to the individual. (Observe that every commodity listed on the form for this project is available for the final user or consumer only because transportation has made it so.) 

Transportation is as close to us as our own stomachs. 

2. No. 57. To emphasize the various kinds of available transportation facilities, including some that are out of the ordinary. (This project is high in interest values.) 

3. No. 58. To localize the students' consideration of transportation.

Important: Units 13 and 14 taken together mark the beginning of a twofold treatment of the several great fields of business activity. At least two, and sometimes three, units are devoted to the treatment of each of the following: Transportation; Communication; Finance and Banking; Insurance; 

Production; and Distribution (Trade)

The first unit in each series usually traces the historical development, and invariably emphasizes the economic importance and social significance of the business activity under consideration, as, for example, transportation. The succeeding unit (or units) as, for example, Unit 14, "Transportation- 

Opportunities and Services," has three separate and distinct phases as follows: 

a. The vocational opportunities and promotional possibilities are pointed out with special emphasis on the clerical group of occupations. The education, training, experience, and other qualifications that workers in the field under consideration must have in order to find employment and win promotion are stressed. Finally, the facilities for obtaining the required education and training are specified. b. The particular business activity under consideration is then considered from the point of view of the individual user or consumer. For example, in Unit 14 the student studies transportation from the point of view of the individual user or consumer of transportation facilities. 

(See "The Traveler's Use of Transportation Facilities and 

Services," page 232, to "The Shipper's Use of Transportation 

Facilities and Services," page 241.) c. After the use of transportation from the point of view of the individual consumer and citizen has been considered, the student is then taken "behind the counter," so to speak, and shown how transportation is used in business, and, whenever possible, is also placed in the position of a clerical worker in the business office of an organization engaged in the particular kind of business activity under consideration. Keeping these features in mind will be especially helpful in teaching Units 13-26 inclusive.

Answers to Test, Research, and Discussion Questions for Unit 13 

1. Principally water, pack-animal, and vehicular transportation. 

The efficiency of water transportation was greatly increased by the invention of the steam engine.

The first modern invention that brought about the need for roads was the automobile. However, the transportation of armies and war equipment gave rise to considerable road building in earlier times. The Appian 

Way in ancient Rome is an example. The Old National Trail, extending from Washington, D. C., westward across the Allegheny Mountains, is also an example of road building for the accommodation of vehicular traffic before the invention of the automobile. 

Before the invention of the steam engine, boats were propelled by sails or by oars. 

2. The invention of the steam engine made railroads possible. Suitable tracks, brakes, the proper kind of wheels, and the swiveling truck had to be developed in order to make the railroad a practicable means of transportation. 

No, railroads also use electric locomotives.

George Westinghouse invented the air brakes now used for stopping railroad trains. 

3. For a brief description of the development of water transportation, see pages 210-214.

The Atlantic and Pacific oceans are connected by the Panama Canal. 

Before this canal was built, vessels plying between the eastern and western coasts of the United States had to travel around South America through the Strait of Magellan.

The fastest of modern steamships now cross the Atlantic Ocean in only a little more than four days. 

4. No, automobiles could not have been used extensively fifty years ago because good roads were not available. 

More automobiles are used in the United States than in all the rest of the countries of the world taken together. 

The use of automobiles is less practicable in Asia, Africa, and South America because there are not so many good roads in those sections of the world. The automobile is a more efficient means of transportation than the railroad, in the sense that it does not limit transportation to specially constructed tracks. It is a more facile and flexible means of transportation than the railroad. 

5. Wright Brothers invented the first practicable airplane.

In one sense, airplanes first came to practical use during the World War. There may be some differences of opinion among the students as to whether this is a practical use, however. 

In general, airplanes were first used as a transportation facility in the United States for the purpose of carrying mail between Washington, D. C., and New York, New York.

The airplane is a heavier-than-air machine, whereas the dirigible is a lighter-than-air machine.

Airplanes are far more extensively used for transportation at present. 

For a brief statement of the extent to which electricity has aided in the development of transportation, see pages 224-225. There is almost no limit to the variety of possible answers to this question. 

In a general sense, a common carrier is any agency of transportation or means used to transport merchandise or people for hire.

Lippincott Company.

Company.

& Sons.

Answers to Test, Research, and Discussion Questions for Unit 14 

1. Yes, there are more opportunities for boys than for girls. 

This is not necessarily as true in the business offices as it is in the operating or construction departments. 

No, the duties would not be fundamentally different. 

2. The answers to the first five parts will depend upon local conditions, personal opinions, etc.

Personal services extended by railroads to travelers are enumerated on pages 232-239.

An airplane is undoubtedly the fastest means of transportation between New York and San Francisco.

By train, the journey requires approximately four days, depending on routes, rapidity of trains used, and other factors. 

By steamship, the journey requires from ten days to two weeks. 

The distance between New York and San Francisco is 3,180 miles.

Answers to the last two parts of this question will depend upon locality. 

Under ordinary circumstances, steamship service must be used in traveling from anywhere in the United States to either Europe or Japan. However, the airplane, and more particularly the dirigible, have been used by a few people for traveling between the United States and Europe. 

3. Goods may be shipped by freight, parcel post, and express. 

Express, or under some conditions parcel post, is the quickest way. 

Freight is the slowest. Express or parcel post (sometimes one and sometimes the other, depending on distance and other conditions) is the most expensive. Freight is the cheapest.

A small package should be sent by parcel post. 

If it were very valuable, it should be sent by express, because of the greater factors of safety.

The lumber dealer would probably make the shipment to the Boston customer by boat freight.

The Chicago meat packer would use fast freight and the refrigerator car. 

The refrigeration service would be required for the meat shipment, but not for a flour shipment.

4. Seventy pounds.

No, the weight limit beyond Zone 3 is 50 pounds. 

Yes, a parcel-post package weighing 50 pounds or less may be shipped from any point in the United States to Honolulu. 

A maximum of 15 pounds may be shipped to Canada by parcel post, but customs regulations must be complied with. 

Collection at the point of shipment and delivery at destination are available to shippers who use railway express service, but not to those who ship by railroad freight.

Generally speaking, express service is faster than parcel post, as well as safer. Express companies also call for packages that are to be shipped by express, but the post-office authorities do not do so. 

To insure delivery of a parcel-post package in the shortest possible time, special-delivery service should be used. 

Insurance service protects the shipper against the loss of parcel-post packages.

C.O.D. service is the practice of the United States post office and of express companies of collecting from the consignee an amount stipulated by the shipper of a package before the package is turned over to the consignee. 

No, railroads do not accept C.O.D. freight shipments.

The consignee of a freight shipment may, however, be compelled to pay for it if the shipper consigns it to a bank or to some other party and uses an order bill of lading for the purpose. (See "C.O.D. Shipments," pages 256-257, for specific answers to the last part of this question.) 

5. Time, weight, value, cost, etc. must be considered in deciding how to have incoming and outgoing shipments made.

No, railroads must submit proposed increases in passenger and freight rates to the Interstate Commerce Commission of the United States Government and have them approved before they can be put into effect. 

This applies only to railroads that do an interstate business. A railroad operated wholly within the boundary lines of one state would not have to conform to this regulation, but, in most states, it would have to submit such proposals to the proper state government authority, such as the 

Public Service Commission or the Public Utilities Commission. 

In a like manner, street-car companies cannot charge any other rates than those approved by some city or state authority, such as the Public Utilities Commission. Sometimes rates are stipulated in the franchise under which the street-car company operates. Answers to this and the preceding question can be amplified to a considerable extent.

Bibliography Chisholm, G. G., "Handbook of Commercial Geography," Long mans, Green & Company.

Jones, Eliot, "Principles of Railway Transportation," The Mac millan Company.

I. AIMS: 

UNIT 15—COMMUNICATION  

To understand the development of communication and to appreciate its functions in modern economic life. (See "Ob jective," Unit 15, Table of Contents.) 

Note: The treatment of the basic business activity, communication, in Units 15, 16, and 17 follows the same general plan as the treatment of transportation in Units 13 and 14. 

It will, therefore, be helpful to read again what is said about "New Features of Method and Subject Matter," on page 125 of this manual.

Unit 15 traces the historical development of communication and emphasizes its social and economic implications. Guidance, exploration, and precise vocational practice in properly balanced proportions characterize the treatment of communication in Units 16 and 17. Certain of the projects focus the student's attention on the communication facilities in his own community and on his daily experiences in using them. 

1. To trace briefly the historical development of modern communication facilities, thus building essential knowledges, appreciations, and attitudes. 

2. To develop an understanding of the social and economic importance of communication. 

3. Other specific aims will depend largely on daily assignments and developments during recitations.

C. PROJECT AIMS (see Project Titles): 

No. 66. To focus the student's attention on the communication facilities and services in his own community. 

II. MATERIALS:

Postal Guide.

Note: In the transition process, it is particularly important that students be encouraged to do most of the talking. It should be a kind of "drawing-out" process. As a result of their own personal experiences, students have a wealth of information at their command. It is the function of the transition process to draw out that knowledge and show its application.

Answers to Test, Research, and Discussion Questions for Unit 15 1. Communication includes every means by which messages, spoken or written, are conveyed from one person to another. . (See page 266 and amplify.)

Face-to-face conversation is no doubt the simplest form of communication. (See page 266.)

2. Adhesive stamps were first used in England in 1840. (See page 268.)

Benjamin Franklin, first as Postmaster in Philadelphia and later as 

Deputy Postmaster-General of the British Colonies of North America, developed facilities for communication between the colonies. (See page 271.) 

The pony express operated between St. Joseph, Missouri, and San 

Francisco, California.

It was discontinued because the development of railroad and telegraph lines provided more efficient transportation and communication facilities. 

3. The telegraph first made use of electricity for communication. 

Samuel F. B. Morse was the inventor of the telegraph.

Prior to the invention of the telegraph, communication depended almost wholly on transportation facilities because written messages had to be transported from the place of origin to destination, or from the sender to the receiver.

The submarine cable grew directly out of the invention of the telegraph.

The invention and development of the submarine cable provided a means for quick communication between Europe and America. Before the first cable between the two continents was laid, the only available facility for communication between the continents was by means of written or verbal messages transported by slow vessels. 

4. Alexander Graham Bell invented and perfected the telephone.

The first "long-distance" telephone message was sent from Boston to 

Cambridge, Massachusetts.

In telephoning, the listener actually hears the voice of the speaker. In telegraphing, the sender's message is transmitted in written form to the receiver in that form.

Long-distance telephone conversation between Boston and San Francisco was first made possible in 1915. 

5. Wireless telegraphy is an important aid to navigation because it affords the only means of communication with ships at sea. If equipped with "wireless," ships in distress can call for help from other vessels, and there are many other obvious advantages. 

Mail, cablegram, telephone, and radiogram. 

Mail is the cheapest. By the terms of the International Postal Agreement, letters between any point in the United States and the cities mentioned in this question require only the regular postage. 

Telephone service is the most expensive. 

Telephone service was most recently made available.

Lothrop, Lee & Shepard Company.

1. Collins, F. A., "The Wireless Man," The Century Company.

UNIT 16-COMMUNICATION-OPPORTUNITIES AND SERVICES I. AIMS: 

A. GENERAL AIM (for Units 16 and 17): 

To learn to use mail, telephone, and telegraphic services and to provide for exploration and practice in the field of communication. (See "Objective," Unit 16, Table of Contents.)

1. To learn about the opportunities for employment and the possibilities for promotion in the field of communication. (Also applicable to Unit 17.) 

2. To learn about the education and training, and the available facilities for acquiring them, needed in order to render acceptable service and win promotion in the field of communication. (Also applicable to Unit 17.) 

3. To learn the fundamentals of business letter writing and to lay a foundation for the subject of business English and correspondence in the business course on the senior high school level. 

4. To learn how to handle incoming and outgoing mail. 

5. To acquire postal information and to learn how to use it. 

6. Other specific aims will depend largely on daily assignments and developments during recitations.

4. No. 70. To learn to determine mail classifications and calculate postage rates.

Because when window envelopes are used the name and address appear on the insertion and not on the envelope; if the insertion is not properly made, all or a part of the name and address may be obscured.

Obviously, the knowledge required to answer such questions as the preceding is of practical use to everyone. Reasons given by students for their answers will, of course, vary. From the educator's point of view, the final outcome of such knowledge is an appreciation and understanding of communication as the division of business that provides everyone with facilities for conveying messages. There is no more essential factor in the whole scheme of civilization than adequate, efficient communication facilities.
