III. PREPARATION AND APPROACH (Teacher Activities): 

Review and summary of the lessons for each day.

1. Draw heavily on the students' everyday experiences in the use of the telephone. Student experiences in the use of the telegram, cablegram, and radiogram will be comparatively rare, but in almost any class there will be some students whose relatives or friends have sent or received telegrams and possibly cablegrams and radiograms as well. Such experiences are all the more valuable because of their comparative rarity. 

2. Have students mention personal acquaintances that may be employed by telephone or telegraph companies and describe briefly the duties they perform. (Boys may know telegraph messengers, etc., and girls may have friends who are employed as telephone operators.)

C. PRETESTS: 

1. This is by the teacher; the last part of question 1, page 345, the third part of question 2, the second and third parts of question 3, and the fifth part of question 5 may be used in the pretesting process. 

2. For skill in finding information in a telephone directory, Projects Nos. 71 and 73 may be used as a part of this process before the projects are assigned for preparation. 

3. Formal pretests if the teacher desires to prepare them. 

A. TRANSITION: 

1. Have students refer to completed Project No. 64 and explain that in No. 76 exactly the same kind of questions are asked but that those questions now refer specifically to the field of communication. This is part of the vocational-guidance aspect of this unit. 

2. Have students refer to the two forms for Project No. 65. 

Those who completed No. 65 need not work out No. 77. 

Furthermore, only those to whom communication appeals as a desirable field for employment need work out No. 77. 

3. Ask such questions as these:

What communication facilities are widely used by newspapers in gathering news from all over the world? (The telegram, cablegram, and radiogram.)

If you were away from home and were suddenly taken ill, how would you notify the members of your family? (By telegram or telephone.) 

How would the captain of a ship disabled by storm at sea call for help? (By radiogram.)

The versatile teacher will think of many other questions that will further the purpose of the transition process. 

1. Have students discuss topics previously assigned for preparation as homework or supervised study. 

2. Call on students for prepared answers to test, research, and discussion questions. 

3. Call on students for discussion of completed business projects, with special emphasis on No. 72.

Note: Project No. 72 represents an approved and an effective method of developing the desirable character trait of courtesy. 

The expressions in the left-hand column of the form for this project are incorrect, but do not tell the students that these statements are incorrect! Simply tell them that there is a

better, more efficient, and more effective way of answering incoming and making outgoing telephone calls. This lesson in courtesy is dramatized and there is no "preaching" in it. The outcomes of this project are (1) courtesy and (2) skill development. 

4. The illustrations in this unit may be used to good advantage as the basis of teaching situations and student discussions. 

Note: The illustration on page 320 shows a city street thirty years ago; page 321 shows the same street today. Emphasize the fact that business enterprise and inventive genius working together removed the maze of wires from the poles and placed them in underground conduits in less than thirty years. It is more efficient and more economical to lay wires in underground conduits than to put them on poles-the business point of view. It is also much more pleasant and convenient for the citizens the social point of view. The conclusion is that business efficiency and public welfare often go hand in hand. 

5. In this unit it is especially important that students be called upon to express their vocational preferences. It is just as important for the student to decide that he would not like to prepare for a career in the field of communication as it is for him to select this field for future employment. The needs of the drop-out group must also be kept in mind, and for that reason most of the vocational opportunities in this treatment of communication are confined to a consideration of those on the junior level of occupation.

7. Call on students for definitions of words and expressions. 

8. Call for students' reports on any supplementary reading that may have been assigned. 

9. Make free use of the blackboard for teaching telephone courtesy, for summarizing the points involved in the efficient use of the telephone, for listing the duties of workers in the field of communication as described in this unit, and for listing the various classifications of telegraph and cable services.

Remedial teaching is, therefore, left to the teacher's judgment and discretion.

Answers to Test, Research, and Discussion Questions for Unit 17 

1. Telephone, telegram, cablegram, and radiogram.

Each of these four communication facilities utilizes electricity in the transmission of messages. Most other forms of communication depend upon some kind of transportation facility for the transmission of messages. 

In telephone communication, the voice of the speaker is actually heard by the listener. In the telegram, cablegram, and radiogram, however, the message is reduced to writing and transmitted in that form. 

2. Two-party line telephone service would be unsatisfactory in a large business office because the line might be in use by one party when it was badly needed by the other. 

The telephone directory contains: (1) a complete list of telephone subscribers alphabetically arranged, their street addresses and telephone numbers, (2) in most cases a classified business directory, and (3) general instructions to telephone users.

Street addresses are given in the telephone directory because it helps to locate and identify a particular subscriber. In the directories of large cities, it is not unusual to find two subscribers having the same name and initials. In such cases the street address helps to distinguish one from the other. Furthermore, the presence of street addresses in the telephone directory is a great convenience in other ways. 

See pages 319-322.

The out-of-town call costs more. For the difference between a stationto-station and a person-to-person call, see page 322 of the text. 

Three specific jobs in a large telephone exchange are: (1) telephone operator, (2) chief operator, (3) information clerk. 

3. The telegraph was invented before the telephone. 

Undoubtedly, the usual response to this question will be "the telephone."

The telephone is of greater use to more individuals than the telegraph. 

For the four kinds of telegraph service, see pages 333-334. Also refer to the telegraph blanks supplied by telegraph companies. Slightly different terminology is used by different companies in describing the same class of service. 

The standard telegram is the fastest kind of service.

The night message is the least expensive for messages containing not more than ten words.

For long messages, the night letter is the least expensive. 

For the different ways in which telegraph messages may be filed for transmission, see page 336.

The “report-delivery" service should be used in order to make sure that a telegram has been delivered.

The "repeat-back" service should be used to make sure that a telegram was transmitted exactly as worded by the sender. 

4. The Topeka business man could file his cablegram at any telegraph office. Either Western Union or Postal Telegraph will accept cables of this kind.

For the four kinds of cable service, see page 340. The terminology used by different companies for describing these four classes of service is not absolutely uniform. 

The standard full-rate service is the fastest. 

The week-end letter service is the cheapest.

To meet the conditions in the fifth part of this question, either the deferred half-rate or the cable-letter service might be used. 

No, neither of these two services could be used for messages in code. 

In counting the words in a cablegram, all words, letters, and figures in both the address and the signature are counted. In telegrams, however, neither the address nor the signature is taken into consideration when counting the words in the message. 

5. Code messages are used in order to reduce cost and insure secrecy. 

No, such code systems do not insure secrecy-they only reduce the cost. 

Wireless telegraphy enables ships at sea to send and receive messages. 

That would not be possible with the telegraph or the submarine cable. 

Recently, however, special wireless telephone equipment has been installed in a few ships so that it is possible to carry on telephone conversations

between such ships and certain points on land. This is still a very rare thing.

The Radio Corporation of America supplies most of the radio communication service between the United States and foreign countries. 

Bibliography 

Meriwether, Susan, "The Story of the Telephone," Harper &

Brothers. 

1. Hemingway, Alfred T., "How to Make Good," The Gregg Pub lishing Company. 

2. Casson, Herbert N., "History of the Telephone," A. C. McClurg & Company.

Answers to Test, Research, and Discussion Questions for Unit 18 1. Many things have been used as mediums of exchange. Examples are wampum, elk teeth, polished pebbles, gun powder, animals, etc., in addition to such metals as gold, copper, and silver. All these things served the same purpose as our modern money. 

Many considerations have always entered into the selection of the things that have been used as mediums of exchange. Desirability, however, has always been one important factor. Such metals as gold, copper, and silver were probably chosen in the beginning because they were soft enough so that designs could be imprinted on them, as well as because they were desirable. Difficulty of imitation was another reason. This is an interesting question and is prolific in opportunities for discussion. 

2. Variable responses may be expected to this question. The following are examples: United States, the dollar; England, the pound sterling; 

France, the franc; Germany, the mark.

Exchange rates fluctuate. The standard exchange value of the English pound sterling is approximately $4.86. The French franc and the German mark were formerly worth 19.3 and 23.8 cents respectively in United

States money, but since the World War the exchange rate has decreased very substantially and continues to fluctuate. Hence, this question may

be made an interesting topic for investigation. The financial pages of most newspapers quote foreign exchange rates and it is, therefore, suggested that students be required to obtain current information in answering this question.

Almost any bank will exchange foreign money for United States money at the current exchange rate.

A small fee would probably be charged for this service because the bank, in turn, would be compelled to have the foreign money exchanged into 

United States money.

No, the man in question would not have to accept $1,000 either in pennies or in nickels. The laws of the United States define what constitutes legal tender. (See page 398 of the text. This is a good pretesting question.) 

3. Such types of business enterprises usually keep money that is received after banks have closed in safes. Some of the banks in the large cities, however, actually collect such money at regular intervals after closing hours. Armored cars, with which many students are familiar, call for the money and convey it to the bank vaults. 

Responses will vary, but in many communities banks oper before 10 a.m. or remain open in the late afternoon and early evening in order to serve people who, for various reasons, cannot go to the bank during regular hours.

The cost of the privilege of borrowing money is called interest; in other words, interest is the cost of credit from the borrower's point of view. 

From the lender's point of view, interest is what credit earns. (Avoid establishing the unsound concept that interest is "money charged for the use of money.")

For the difference between state banks and national banks, see pages 356-357 of the text. National banks operate under the supervision and control of the United States Government, while state banks operate under the supervision and control of state governments. 

A checking account is more convenient than a savings account because funds can be withdrawn from the savings bank only by presenting the savings deposit book to the bank, whereas with a checking account checks can be issued to any payee. (See page 360 of the text.) 

Two common causes of bank failures are: (1) lending too much money belonging to depositors which cannot be collected promptly enough to provide for heavy withdrawals of deposits, and (2) sudden and unexpected withdrawals of funds by depositors that equal or nearly equal the amount of money actually in the bank's vaults or readily obtainable. 

Occasionally, embezzlement of large sums by employees causes failure, and there are many other reasons. 

4. From building and loan associations, cooperative banks, savings banks, and similar institutions that specialize in such loans.

In practically all cases a mortgage would have to be given as security. 

Yes. In fact, many states supervise such special institutions even more closely than they do ordinary banks. 

A first mortgage takes precedence over a second mortgage. For example, if mortgaged property is sold, a first mortgage must be paid off before the second mortgage. A second mortgage is inferior to a first mortgage with respect to its degree of security. 

The first mortgage is the safer investment, but the second mortgage sometimes yields a higher return. 

The rate of interest on a second mortgage is usually higher because the risk is greater. 

5. Generally speaking, an investment that yields 4 per cent is safer than one that pays 12 per cent.

Interest coupons are attached to unregistered bonds. At the interest period the owner detaches the proper coupon and, on presentation of this coupon, receives the interest due on the bond. 

In most cases, a bond would be sold through a local broker, but certain bonds might also be sold through banks.

Yes, a fee would be charged for disposing of the bond. 

No, B, C, D, and E could not elect the members of the board of directors in this case in opposition to A's wishes. 

Yes, A could select the board of directors.

A would control this situation because he owns more than half the stock.

UNIT 19-FINANCE AND BANKING-OPPORTUNITIES AND SERVICES I. AIMS: 

To learn to use banking services and to provide for exploration and clerical practice in the field of banking and finance. 

(See "Objective," Unit 19, Table of Contents.)

1. To learn about the opportunities for employment and the possibilities for promotion in the field of banking and finance. 

2. To learn about the education and training and the facilities for acquiring them that are needed in order to render acceptable service and to win promotion in the field of banking and finance. 

3. To learn to use banking facilities and services-the consumer point of view. 

4. To learn to perform the duties of workers on the junior level of occupation in the field of finance and banking—the distributor point of view. 

5. To learn the functions of business documents used in connection with financial activities and to get some practice in the preparation of such documents-exploration and vocational practice. 

6. Other specific aims will depend largely upon daily assignments and developments during recitations.

Project No. 82 provide variety.

5. No. 86. To learn the functions of cashier's checks and bank drafts and to obtain some practice in their preparation.

7. No. 88. To learn the purpose of and to obtain some practice in preparing receipts. 

8. No. 89. To learn the procedure in transferring funds by telegraph and to obtain some practice in the preparation of the forms used. 

9. No. 90. To learn the function of a collateral loan note and to obtain some practice in its preparation. 

10. No. 91. To learn the function and form of promissory notes and to obtain some practice in their preparation. 

11. No. 92. To learn the form and function of commercial drafts and to obtain some practice in their preparation.

12. No. 93. To learn the form and function of a merchant's bill and the trade acceptance in relation to each other and to obtain some practice in the preparation of each. 

13. No. 94. To learn something about the duties of the pay-roll clerk and to obtain some practice in the performance of those duties and in the preparation of forms used by the pay-roll clerk. 

14. No. 95. Vocational guidance. 

15. No. 96. Vocational and educational guidance.

Answers to Test, Research, and Discussion Questions for Unit 19 1. A bank's principal source of income is the interest charged on loans. 

Loans are made from the funds that depositors leave with the bank for safekeeping. It costs a bank something to safeguard depositors' funds and to keep the necessary records. Hence, if a depositor does not keep a certain reasonable amount on deposit, a bank has no way of earning the income necessary to pay the expense for taking care of that depositor's funds. Not all banks follow this practice, but most of the large ones do. 

Pennies are usually wrapped in standard-sized packages of 50 cents; nickels in packages of $2; dimes in packages of $5; quarters in packages of $10; and half dollars in packages of $10 or $20. 

The identification number referred to in this part of question 1 is assigned the bank in question by the clearing house and helps to facilitate the transaction of its business with all other banks. According to one plan, all cities or districts in the United States are given numbers, and each bank within the particular district is given a number of its own. 

2. In such cases, the depositor should make out a duplicate copy of the deposit slip and have it receipted by the receiving teller.

Ordinarily, the proceeds of an out-of-town check may not be withdrawn before the bank in which it has been deposited has made the collection from the bank on which it is drawn. 

In some states, the law imposes the penalty of a jail or prison sentence for issuing checks that result in overdrawing a depositor's account. 

Conditions in different states vary, however, and answers will not be uniform. Local information may be secured from any bank. 

By keeping an accurate and up-to-date record of all deposits made and checks issued. The check-stub record serves this purpose. 

If a check is made payable "to the order of" someone, the title to it may be transferred from one party to another by indorsement. Obviously, this is a great convenience, both to the drawer and the payee of a check. 

Checks indorsed in blank may be cashed by anyone into whose possession they fall, which, of course, may be contrary to the intention of the drawer. 

3. The check-stub record and the bank statement should be reconciled at least once each month.

If a check is lost, the drawer should notify the bank on which it is drawn to stop payment.

No, this practice is not advisable. The practice of postdating checks is always objectionable.

Responses will vary. Under such circumstances, a check might be cashed at some place where the payee is known and trusted. 

4. Firms are often compelled to refuse such requests because not all checks are "good." Many business firms have been defrauded in this way and they are naturally cautious about cashing checks. 

For the difference between a certified check, a cashier's check, and a bank draft, see pages 395-400 of the text. The certified check is drawn by the depositor but certified by the cashier or other officer of the bank on which it is drawn; the cashier's check is a check drawn by the cashier or other officer on the bank with which such official is connected; a bank draft is really a check by one bank on another bank. 

Yes, the creditor might refuse to accept a check under such circumstances, but if it were a certified check, he would, naturally, be willing to accept it under most circumstances. The law does provide, however, for payment of debts in legal tender. (See pages 398–399 of the text.)

A postal money order is less convenient than a bank check because it can be obtained only by going to the post office and making out the necessary application. The money order is also less convenient for the payee. 

The telegraph company will accept a personal check only from an individual who has a credit rating with the telegraph company. 

While traveling in the United States, American Express Company or 

American Bankers' Association traveler's checks are the most convenient.

For traveling in Europe, American Express Company traveler's check is

also satisfactory, but many travelers use letters of credit. (See pages 

403-404 of the text.) 

5. A secured loan is one in which property of some kind is pledged as security for repayment. If the loan is not paid, property so placed becomes the property of the lender. In an unsecured loan, there is no pledging of specific property as security.

Probably not without investigation by the bank. If a bank has money to lend, a borrower with ample high-grade securities to pledge as collateral is usually investigated. If the results of the investigation are satisfactory, negotiations for the loan are started. The point in this question is that before making loans, banks will usually investigate the reputation of unknown people who want to borrow.

No, the drawee of a draft is not compelled to accept it. A draft is really a request to agree to pay or, in other words, to accept. 

A collection draft is most frequently used as a means of collecting loans or overdue accounts. In trade acceptances, however, the obligation on the part of the drawee invariably arises out of the purchase of a specific lot of goods from the drawer. A trade acceptance is a direct and unqualified promise to pay for a particular bill of goods. (See pages 405-409 of the text.)

No, fundamentally, a promissory note and an acceptance are both promises to pay a specific sum of money to a particular person at a specified time.

Answers to Test, Research, and Discussion Questions for Unit 21 

1. See page 433; also pages 422-423. 

2. For the three kinds of life insurance and the purpose of each kind, see pages 435–436.

The ordinary or straight life policy is the best for this man. 

The young man ought to take out a $5,000 ten-year endowment policy. (Have students calculate cost of a $5,000 endowment policy by referring to the table on page 438.)

3. The total premiums on a twenty-year endowment policy for $1,000 taken out at age 20 would be twenty times $39.79, or $795.80.

A twenty-payment life policy would cost twenty times $20.27, or $405.40.

The mortality table indicates that the insurance company could expect to collect 43 annual premiums (the life expectancy is 42.20 years). 

The total sum would be 43 times $13.03, or $560.29. 

4. See "The Cash-Surrender, Loan, and Paid-Up Insurance Features of

Life Insurance Policies," pages 439-440. 

Ten dollars deposited each month in a savings account for a period of ten years would result in an accumulation of $1,200 plus the accumulated interest. Ten annual life insurance premiums of $120 each would result in the accumulation of a cash-surrender value that would be very much smaller than $1,200, because all but a comparatively small part of each annual premium pays for the service of protection. 

Insurance companies do not issue policies on the lives of people who cannot reasonably be expected to live during the period of life expectancy. 

The person of, say, twenty-five years of age, for example, who is suffering from an incurable disease could not be expected to live for 38.81 years, which is the normal expectation of life for a person of that age. 

5. See "The Cost of Fire Insurance," page 442.

The answer to the last part of this question would depend entirely upon the terms of the policy. However, unless the terms of the policy specifically exempted such items as the loss of this destroyed currency, the insurance would probably cover at least a substantial part of the loss.

Answers to Test, Research, and Discussion Questions for Unit 22 

1. Production includes all processes by which goods and services are obtained and made ready for use. (See opening sentence, page 450, for detailed answers to all parts of this question.) 

2. Yes, the farmer is a producer.

Machinery, farm equipment, buildings, household furnishings, seed, fertilizer, etc.

Responses to the third part of this question will vary. 

So will responses to the last part of the question. 

3. The spinning jenny was invented by James Hargreaves in 1760. 

The power loom was invented by Edmund Cartwright in 1785. 

Undoubtedly, the inventors were prompted by a desire to relieve workers of drudgery and to speed up production. 

These two inventions were both used in manufacturing textiles for clothing, etc. 

4. By the "Industrial Revolution" is usually meant the beginning of the use of machinery to facilitate production. 

The purpose of machinery is to speed up and to lower the cost of production, and to relieve workers of drudgery. 

Manufacturing cost includes the cost of: (1) raw materials, (2) labor, and (3) manufacturing expenses. 

5. Wood, graphite, paint, glue, metal, rubber, etc. are used in the manufacture of a lead pencil.

Wood was obtained by lumbering; graphite, metals, and paint pigments by mining; paint oils by agriculture; glue by fishing or farming; rubber by agriculture, etc.

Flour, yeast, salt, sugar, water, milk, lard, etc. are the raw materials used in baking bread. 

Machinery, ovens, pans, tables, counters, etc. are used in bakeries.

Lowering the cost of production without decreasing selling price and without increasing wages is the selfish way to increase profits. 

Sharing a part of increased profit resulting from reducing the cost of production is sound economics in the broadest sense. (See page 459.)

1. Parsons, F. W., "Everybody's Business," Doubleday, Doran & Company. 

2. Whitbeck, R. H. and Finch, V. C., "Economic Geography," 

McGraw-Hill Book Company, Inc. 

3. Gras, N. S. B., "An Introduction to Economic History," Harper and Brothers.

Emphasize the importance of Nos. 106 and 107. If time is available, a brief history of production and manufacturing might be submitted as special reports for extra credit by superior students. The Industrial Revolution is an interesting field for investigation. 

4. The illustrations on pages 464 and 468 can be used as the basis of interesting teaching situations and student discussions. 

Have students bring in pictures of local factories, etc. The illustration on page 468 shows the interdependence of production and transportation. The one on page 464 shows how the use of machinery lightens man's burdens and enables him to produce more at a lower cost. 

5. Stress the importance of expressing vocational preferences by students, especially in connection with Project No. 109.

1. Vocabulary test based on Unit 23. (See Unit 29.) 2. Objective Test No. 23.

3. Individual reports are especially important in the units dealing with opportunities and services in the various divisions of business activity (Units 14, 16, 17, 19, 21, 23, 25, and 26).

Projects Nos. 108 and 109 in this unit are more important than they would seem at first glance. Considerably more than half of the students who drop out of school before graduating from senior high school enter industrial work. To such students, an appreciation and understanding of production as one of the basic business enterprises is especially helpful. Their study of the business forms used in projects that are a part of the other units will likewise be helpful. Employment managers and personnel directors often point to the fact that the very first promotion out of the ranks of day labor to supervisory work involves the use of a variety of business forms. 

The need for this must be determined from tests and daily classroom events.

1. Farming, lumbering, fishing, mining, and manufacturing. 

Because an understanding and an appreciation of production help people to be more intelligent users or consumers.

They also help students to make intelligent decisions in choosing a career. (See second paragraph, page 462.) 

2. Production offers numerous and varied opportunities for employment on the three levels of occupation. 

Yes, the opportunities for skilled and unskilled workmen exceed those for clerical workers in number. Most of the work in the field of production is done by such workers.

See page 463 for various kinds of work in the business offices of production agencies.

No. Fundamentally, the duties of office workers are much the same in every kind of business activity, although forms may differ. 

3. Responses will not be uniform. However, students may mention such things as radios, safety razors, adding machines, mechanical pencils, electrical household appliances, airplanes, etc.

Things that are cheaper today than twenty-five years ago include such items as silk products, typewriters, automobiles, household electrical appliances, etc.

Simple manufacturing operations conducted in the home include such things as baking, sewing, cooking, canning and preserving, etc.

No. Strictly speaking, selling and delivering finished products are a part of the activities of distribution and not of production. Distribution is treated in Units 24-26.

UNIT 24-TRADE: PURCHASING AND SELLING I. AIMS: 

To understand the purpose of purchasing and selling and to appreciate the economic function of trade or distribution. 

(See "Objective," Unit 24, Table of Contents.)  

1. To emphasize briefly the social implications of distribution or trade as a basic business activity. 

2. To help students to understand the economic importance both to the institution of business and to the individual of thrifty purchasing and efficient selling. 

3. Other specific aims will depend largely on developments during daily recitations.

C. PROJECT AIMS (see Project Titles): 

1. No. 110. To localize the student's consideration of purchasing as one of the two major aspects of trade or distribution. 

2. No. 111. To emphasize purchasing as one of the important activities of business enterprises. 

3. No. 112. To develop powers of observation and analysis as a common, everyday business event. 

4. No. 113. To provide an opportunity to use good judgment based on observation and analysis of display advertisements. 

5. No. 114. To focus the student's attention on the facilities for distributing business goods and services in his own community. 

II. MATERIALS: 

1. The text, project pad, and manual. 

2. Additional materials: a city directory and, if available, some text on merchandising or selling.

B. FOR THE STUDENTS (Primary): 

1. The textbook, project pad, and notebook. 

2. Other materials: any booklets or pamphlets that give information about purchasing, selling, or advertising.

1. Class library. See II-B-2 above. 

Note: In addition to booklets and pamphlets, the superior students, student helpers, or class officers may make a collection of the business forms used in the purchasing and selling departments of some local business firms. Such materials will also be useful with Units 25 and 26. 

2. Class exhibit and bulletin board. Any suitable materials as suggested in II-B-2 and II-C-1 above may be used for this purpose.

III. PREPARATION AND APPROACH (Teacher Activities): 

Daily review and summary of preceding lessons. 

1. Ask students to mention things they have purchased lately either for themselves or for their parents. A student who has not bought some of the common goods and services that business provides will seldom be found in any class. Even such common events as paying trolley or bus fares are examples of purchasing business services. Use the experiences thus related

to emphasize the importance of trade or distribution as one of the basic business activities. Without modern facilities for distribution, many of the very common business goods and services that everyone uses would not be available. 

2. Call on students to relate their personal experiences in selling. 

Such experiences will be much rarer than experiences in purchasing, but for that reason will be all the more interesting. 

Boys who have sold newspapers or magazines will be able to tell interesting and helpful stories about their experiences in inducing people to buy, which is selling. Boys who sell some of the well-known weekly or monthly magazines keep records of their business transactions. Descriptions of such records will be interesting and instructive.

C. PRETESTS: 

1. Questions by the teacher; all of question 1 and the first two parts of question 4, page 482, may be used for this purpose. 

2. Formal pretests if the teacher desires to prepare them

A. TRANSITION: 

1. Use the first two paragraphs in this unit in the transition process. 

2. Exhibit a magazine or a newspaper and call attention to the amount of space that is devoted to advertising, which is one method that distributors (sellers) use in communicating with prospective customers (purchasers). Advertising is "printed salesmanship." 

3. Exhibit the classified business section of the local city or telephone directory and explain that one of the important reasons for listing the names of business firms in these books is to tell customers or purchasers where they can buy business goods and services. 

4. Have students turn to the form for Project No. 110 and point out how necessary it is for every individual to know something about buying. Then turn to the form for Project No. 111 and stress the importance of intelligent purchasing in business enterprises. "Essential business knowledge for both the consumer and the business worker" is the theme song of this entire junior business course.

2. Call on students for prepared answers to test, research, and discussion questions.

4. Use Project No. 112 as the basis of a teaching situation. 

5. Encourage students to their best efforts on Project No. 113. 

Use the advertisements that they bring in as the basis for teaching situations. 

6. Call on students for definitions of words and expressions. 

7. Call for students' reports on any supplementary reading that may have been assigned. 

1. Students' summary of preceding daily lessons. 

2. Students' discussion of topics previously assigned for study. 

3. Student responses to test, research, and discussion questions and other questions propounded by the teacher. 

4. Students' discussion of completed projects. 

5. Students' responses to other activities suggested in III and IV above. 

6. Students' reports on any supplementary reading that may have been assigned.

No. 114.

No. 112 requires students to observe the procedure in making a sale, but almost every student will have an opportunity for such observation while making a purchase for himself or for his parents at some local store. Students may also be encouraged to observe door-to-door canvassers at work for the purpose of this project.

Answers to Test, Research, and Discussion Questions for Unit 24 

1. The manufacturer makes what he sells, whereas the merchant buys what he sells. The consumer purchases articles for the purpose of using them. 

2. As used in this unit, trade refers to purchasing and selling goods or commodities for money, either at wholesale or retail. It is the business activity of buying and selling goods.

Yes, the producer of business goods and services must sell them, and he must also buy raw materials, supplies, and services for his own use. 

Yes, purchasing is an important activity in a large factory. Supplies, materials, and services must be bought economically. Factories and

department stores are two types of business enterprises that usually have well-organized purchasing departments. 

3. The student can acquire a knowledge of where to purchase necessities of life only by becoming thoroughly familiar with his own business community. The classified business sections of city and telephone directories, advertisements of local stores, etc. are helpful. 

One way is by personal shopping. Another is by noticing advertisements of local shoe stores. 

4. By advertising in local newspapers, by sending advertising through the mails, and by window displays. 

No, sales clerks in five-and-ten-cent stores do not need so much knowledge and skill as silk salesmen in a department store. Obviously, the silk salesman needs to know as much as possible about silk in general, about the different kinds of silk he has to sell, which kinds will best meet the needs of a customer, etc. About all the five-and-ten-cent store sales clerk has to do is to wrap packages, receive money, and make change.

Undoubtedly, the combination of the four plans represented in the chart on page 479 combined the desirable features of each separate plan. 

5. Advertising is simply one way of inducing people to buy things. 

In the sense used here, selling (or salesmanship) denotes all business activities by means of which people are induced to purchase goods or services. (See page 479.) 

See "Personal Salesmanship," page 481.

UNIT 25-PURCHASING OPPORTUNITIES AND SERVICES I. AIMS: 

To provide for exploration and clerical practice in the field of purchasing. (See "Objective," Unit 25, Table of Contents.)  

1. To learn about the opportunities for employment and the possibilities for promotion in the field of purchasing. 

2. To learn something about the duties of clerical workers in the purchasing departments of business organizations and to get some practice in the performance of such duties. 

3. To become familiar with the functions of common business forms used in connection with purchasing departments and to acquire some skill in the preparation of such forms. 

4. To learn, by means of acquired knowledge and precise clerical practice, whether purchasing is or is not a desirable field for future employment. 

5. Other specific aims will depend largely on developments during daily recitations.

C. PROJECT AIMS (see Project Titles): 

1. No. 115. To gain practice in indexing catalogues-the duty of the order clerk. 

2. No. 116. To acquire knowledge of the function of purchase requisitions and to develop some skill in their preparation—the duties of the stock clerk. 

3. No. 117. To acquire knowledge of the function of purchase orders and to develop skill in their preparation-the duties of the order clerk. 

4. No. 118. To acquire some knowledge of the function of the purchase order register and to develop skill in keeping this record the duties of the order clerk. 

5. No. 119. To acquire some knowledge of the function of the receiving platform record and to develop skill in its use the duties of the receiving clerk. 

6. No. 120. To learn the function of the business form commonly used for reporting irregularities in shipments and to develop some skill in its preparation-the duties of the receiving clerk. 

7. No. 121. To acquire knowledge of the purpose of inventory records and to develop some skill in their preparation-the duties of the stock clerk.

Note: Observe that in the subject matter of this unit and in the correlated practice materials the emphasis is on the functions of commonly used business forms in facilitating the various related activities that are a part of purchasing departments. Observe, too, how the various projects are related-they illuminate an interesting chain of related business events or procedures. 

[II. MATERIALS: 

2. Additional materials: Purchasing department forms and records in actual use will always be interesting to the students.

B. FOR THE STUDENTS (Primary): 

1. Textbook, project pad, and notebook. 

2. Other materials: clippings, advertisements, actual forms, etc. (See II-C-1 in preceding lesson plan.)

1. Class library. See II-B-2 above. 

2. Class exhibit and bulletin board. Considerable materials from those collected by the students as suggested in II-B-2 above.

C. PRETESTS: 

1. This is by the teacher; the second and third parts of question 1, the second part of question 3, the last part of question 4, and all of question 5, pages 504-505, may be used in the pretesting process. 

2. For the ability to interpret the business forms illustrated in this unit. 

3. Formal pretests if the teacher desires to prepare them.

A. TRANSITION: 

1. Present the business form at the bottom of page 486 as a pro forma communication. Many business forms are used simply because they afford a means of communicating requests, orders, etc. briefly and conveniently. Treat the other business forms illustrated in the unit in the same manner. 

Call on students to express in their own language, the messages conveyed by these forms. 

2. Have students compare some of the forms they have brought to class with the forms given in the project pad and with those illustrated in the text. Emphasize the fact that while purchase order blanks, for example, may differ greatly with respect to size, form, arrangement, printed matter, and many other details, the purpose or function of the purchase order is always the same. Teaching business forms in this way will have a much greater carry-over value than any amount of repetitive practice in the preparation of such forms. 

3. Emphasize the importance of absolute accuracy in the preparation of business forms by showing that carelessness might result in a whole series of mistakes. 

4. Display a catalogue of some kind and call attention to the trade names, catalogue numbers, illustrations, instructions for ordering, prices, etc.

2. Call on students for answers to test, research, and discussion questions. 

4. The business forms illustrated in this unit can be used to good advantage as the basis of teaching situations and student discussions. (See IV-A-1 and 2 above.) 

Note: The business forms used for precise practice in this and other units furnish excellent guidance materials, especially because of their vocational implications. Students who are expected to drop out of school may be directed into more intensive clerical practice, thus equipping them with marketable vocational skills on the junior level of occupation. 

5. Call on students to express their vocational preferences-their dislikes as well as their likes. Handling business forms helps to objectify and motivate preparation for office work. It also helps students to decide whether or not they care for such work. 

6. Call on students for definitions of words and expressions.

7. Call on students for reports on any supplementary reading that may have been assigned. 

1. Students' summaries of preceding daily lesson. 

2. Students' discussion of topics previously assigned for study.

Answers to Test, Research, and Discussion Questions for Unit 25 

1. Responses will vary, but the purchasing agent is the occupation referred to here.

The clerk in charge of a receiving department or a stock room would probably be a worker on the senior level of occupation. Assistants would be on the junior level.

In most cases, department store purchasing is done by several buyers, each a specialist. 

2. For duties of the order clerk, see pages 485-486.

Requests to purchase, internal orders to purchase, purchase requisitions, etc.

An order or a request to purchase is an internal communication addressed to the purchasing department asking that certain items be ordered

A purchase order is a communication from one business organization addressed to some other business organization ordering goods. 

A purchase order register is a record of orders that have been placed and of the delivery of such orders. (See pages 492-493.) 

3. For the duties of the receiving clerk, see pages 493-494. 

Extensive technical training is unnecessary for such employment. 

Generally speaking, not many girls are employed in receiving departments. 

See illustrations, pages 495, 497, 498. 

4. For the duties of the stock-room clerk see pages 498-499. 

For the forms used by the stock clerk see pages 499, 500, 502, 503. 

In most cases, no-not without a written order or "requisition for stock." 

5. Undoubtedly, there should be cooperation between the purchasing agent and the sales manager. "Goods well bought are half sold." See pages 465 and 468, 476 and 477, and 478 and 479.

UNIT 26-SELLING-OPPORTUNITIES AND SERVICES I. AIMS: 

To learn the procedures in selling, billing, shipping, and collecting; also, to provide for exploration and clerical practice in the field of selling. (See "Objective," Unit 26, Table of

Contents.)

2. To learn about different kinds of selling work. 

3. To develop, through a study of selling work, some character traits that are helpful to everybody. 

4. To learn to perform the duties of workers in the field of selling, with emphasis on the selling jobs that are on the junior level of occupation. 

5. To learn the functions of business documents used in connection with selling activities and to get some practice in the preparation of such documents.

The advantages of paying cash: greater economy, no debts, etc. 

The advantages of buying on credit: the purchaser has the use of materials bought during the term of credit, greater ease of adjustment if purchases prove unsatisfactory, etc.

UNIT 27-FILING I. AIMS: 

To learn about filing systems, methods, and equipment, and to provide exploration and clerical practice in the field of filing. 

(See "Objective," Unit 27, Table of Contents.)  

1. To learn the function and appreciate the importance of filing as one of the activities in practically all business offices. 

2. To learn something about modern filing equipment and forms. 

3. To learn something about the various methods of filing and the purposes served by each method. 

4. To gain a considerable amount of precise practice in alphabetizing, which is the foundation of all filing. 

5. Other specific aims will depend largely upon developments during daily recitations.

C. PROJECT AIMS (see Project Titles): 

1. No. 131. To provide precise practice in the application of the principles of alphabetizing. 

2. No. 132. To develop knowledge and skill in alphabetizing a list of school subjects, textbooks, and publishers. 

3. No. 133. To develop knowledge and skill in alphabetizing a list of large cities by states, also alphabetically arranged. 

4. No. 134. To show the relation between numeric and alphabetic filing. 

5. No. 135. To develop skill in lettering and ruling. 

6. No. 136. To provide an opportunity to see an actual filing department in operation and, after observation, to write a report thereof.

Note: It will be observed that this project is one of those designated as optional in the "Suggested Assignments of

Business Projects," on pages 31-32 of this manual. It may also be used as a means of rewarding superior attainment, or it may be assigned to superior students if satisfactory completion should entitle students to extra credit. The filing equipment in the school office may be studied by the class. 

II. MATERIALS: 

2. Additional materials: Manufacturers of filing equipment will be glad to send descriptive booklets, circulars, etc. Student helpers or class officers should be assigned the task of obtaining such materials.

B. FOR THE STUDENTS (Primary): 

1. The textbook, project pad, and notebook.

2. Other materials: See II-A-2 above.

Suitable materials selected from those suggested in II-A-2 above. 

2. Class exhibit and bulletin board.

Suitable materials from those suggested in II-A-2 above.

Daily review and summary of preceding lessons. 

1. This will be limited. Call on students to relate any personal experiences they may have had in the use of filing equipment. 

No doubt a number of students will have observed a filing department or at least some filing equipment. 

2. Call on various students to tell about personal acquaintances that may be employed as file clerks. Have them describe briefly the duties of such people. 

C. PRETESTS: 

1. By the teacher. 

2. For skill in filling in and filing cards. 

3. Formal pretests if the teacher desires to prepare them.

1. Use the first three paragraphs in this unit.

3. Emphasize the file clerk's opportunities for learning office details; also, the importance of absolute accuracy, regularity of attendance, the ability to follow instructions, and other character traits. 

4. Review "The Form and Arrangement of Business Letters," 

pages 288-293. (File clerks must be able to identify names, locations, subjects, etc. in the materials they file.) 

2. Call on students for answers to test, research, and discussion questions. 

4. Use the illustrations and forms in this unit as the basis of teaching situations and student discussions. They may also be used for testing powers of observation, accuracy, and skill. 

5. Call on students to express their vocational preferences. 

6. Call on students for definitions of words and expressions. 

7. Call on students for reports on any supplementary reading that may have been assigned.

1. Students' summaries of preceding daily lesson. 

2. Students' discussion of topics previously assigned for study.

4. Students' discussions of completed projects. 

5. Students' responses to other activities suggested in III and IV above. 

6. Students' reports on any supplementary reading that may have been assigned.

UNIT 28-MISCELLANEOUS BUSINESS PROJECTS I. AIMS: 

To provide an opportunity for the application of acquired business knowledge to concrete business situations on the level of the student's experience, and to provide additional educational and vocational guidance. (See "Objective," Unit 

28, Table of Contents.) C. PROJECT AIMS (see Project Titles): 

1. No. 137. To list some of the business problems of the American pioneer. 

2. No. 138. To gather data about important inventions that definitely affected business. 

3. No. 139. To solve, on the student's level, an elementary business problem. 

4. No. 140. To list important facts about various kinds of businesses. 

5. No. 141. To solve some of the business problems of a school athletic association. 

6. No. 142. To make a comparison of the business activities of a school lunch room and a school club with those of a retail store. 

7. No. 143. To make out a customer's comparative rating chart of three retail stores. 

8. No. 144. To secure accurate information relative to the regulation of school attendance and working conditions. 

Note: This group of projects may be assigned for completion at different stages of progress throughout the course, or they may be assigned as a group on completion of Unit 27. The use made of them is left largely to the judgment of the teacher.

The business projects that are a part of each unit of subject matter in this course are designed primarily to afford precise practice that will result in the development of vocational skills. It should be remembered that the development of clerical skills involves a good deal more than repetitive practice in the preparation of bills, invoices, checks, and other common business documents. There is so much variation in the size, form, arrangement, and other aspects of such documents that much

of the repetitive practice provided in the instructional materials used in junior business training courses has very little carry-over value. 

As a matter of fact, filling in any ruled form according to directions is the finest kind of clerical training. Employers constantly emphasize the importance of the ability to follow directions and to use "common sense," of dependability, accuracy, honesty, and other character traits. The business projects that form such an important part of this course cannot be worked out correctly unless the project directions in the text are followed to the letter. The directions and the form for each project constitute a business problem or situation. Recording and tabulating required information according to specific directions is precise clerical practice of the most effective kind.

INSPECTING, CHECKING, AND GRADING COMPLETED BUSINESS PROJECTS 

The business projects, which provide most essential student activities, are also the means of reducing to a minimum the time and effort expended by teachers in inspecting, checking, and grading. Many of the completed projects require no more than very brief inspection for penmanship, neatness, degree of completion, etc. Others require some checking for accuracy of recorded information. Comparatively few require actual grading. 

This elimination of much tedious work outside of regular working hours will be a welcome relief to teachers.

The aim of each project is set forth in the lesson plan based on the unit of which the project in question is a part. (See I-C of each lesson plan.) Sections IV, V, and VI of many of the lesson plans also contain definite suggestions for the presentation and checking of a number of the projects. The use of the projects to conform with the three-level assignment plan and for recognizing and rewarding superior attainment is set forth on pages 7-9 of this manual.

Although it is not the intention of the authors that each completed project be given a numerical or letter grade, teachers who prefer to grade all written work done by the students may find the following general suggestions helpful: 

1. A Grade of Work (90-99). In order to merit an A grade, a project should be complete in every detail. The directions in the

text should be followed to the letter, and penmanship, neatness, and general appearance should be distinctly superior. 

2. B Grade of Work (80-89). In order to merit a B grade, a project should be at least three-fourths completed. The penmanship, neatness, and general appearance should be good. 

3. C Grade of Work (70-79). In order to merit a C grade, a project should be at least one-half, and preferably two-thirds, completed. The penmanship, neatness, and general appearance should at least represent the student's very best effort and show no signs of a careless or indifferent attitude. 

Obviously, the above suggestions are quite general and may be changed to conform with the judgment of the teacher, or disregarded entirely. In fact, many teachers will probably prefer to determine grades by the less burdensome means of objective tests, with the possible addition of a more or less subjective grading of the student's notebook or file of completed projects about twice during each semester of the school year. 

The following comments are intended to supplement suggestions that have already been made and to present the following: 

Outcomes. Many of the projects, especially those in the early part of the course, call for expressions of likes and dislikes, judgments, decisions, attitudes, etc., and for the relation of personal experiences, observations, etc. Naturally, no standardized answers can be set up by which the results of such student activities can be measured objectively. In connection with such projects, an attempt has been made to give the results of experience in using the material; to suggest desirable student responses; and to caution against expecting too much from students for such reasons as lack of maturity, geographical limitations, inadequate reference and other equipment, and other handicaps. 

Suggestions. Whenever practicable, suggested solutions are given for those projects for which standardized answers are not possible. Reference books and other sources of information that students should consult in working out the projects are suggested. 

It should be remembered, however, that suggested solutions are intended to be used only as guides, and not as yardsticks for measuring the finished product of individual students.

Solutions. These consist of uniform or standardized answers whenever such answers are possible. 

The following comments are numbered to correspond with the numbers of the projects as given in the text and on the blank forms. 

No. 1. Individual answers. Therefore, inspect for penmanship, neatness, and degree of completion, but do not check or grade. 

The purpose is to focus the student's attention on the everyday business activities in his business community and to begin the development of an appreciation of business as the social agency that provides people with the things they want and need. Hence, interest and attitudes should be emphasized. Do not be surprised if some students' answers are limited. It may be the first time their attention has been focused on business activities in their community. Encourage students to continue observing business activities at every opportunity. See I-C of the lesson plan for the fourth class period (page 47).

No. 2. Individual answers. Therefore, inspect only for penmanship, neatness, and degree of completion, but do not check or grade. Emphasize importance of thoughtful planning for the future. See IV-B of the lesson plan for the fifth class period 

(page 52). 

No. 3. Individual answers. Inspect but do not check or grade. 

Refer students to page 18 of the text for the kind of questions called for, but encourage the addition of original questions. Ten questions are not too many to insist upon from each student. The teacher, parents, friends, etc. are proper sources to consult for answers to such questions. See V-B-2 of the lesson plan for the sixth class. period (page 55).

No. 4. Individual answers. Inspect briefly, but do not check or grade. Emphasize this as the first of a number of projects that will be helpful in making intelligent plans and decisions about future careers. Do not expect very definite answers-the students are just beginning to "find their way" in this new course. 

No. 5. Individual answers. Inspect for penmanship, neatness, and degree of completion. Check briefly for accuracy of school programs, but do not grade. Emphasize attitudes. Do not penalize students who do not show remarkable improvements in

their budgets of time. See IV-B of the lesson plan for the eighth class period (page 62).

No. 6. Individual answers. Do not inspect, check, grade, or discuss this project after it has been completed. The information called for is of a confidential nature, and it should be explained in advance that the project represents the type of investigation that is made by civil-service commissions, boards of education, and large business establishments in selecting new employees. The principal object of the project is to show that such investigations are made by employers.

No. 7. Individual answers. Inspect, but do not check or grade. 

No doubt many teachers will want to suggest that students fill in the lower right-hand section first, the lower left-hand section next, and so on, proceeding upward according to educational requirements. The superior student ought to fill in the entire thirty blank spaces. The average student ought to fill in at least twenty of the blanks. The poor student should fill in at least twelve or fifteen of them.

Attitudes and appreciations, rather than technical skills or clerical practices, should be emphasized in these early lessons. 

Following directions and filling in ruled forms provide effective clerical training, but this should not be emphasized at this time. 

No. 8. Uniform answers may be expected in Sections 1 and 2 of this project. Information in Section 3 will vary. Information about schools may be obtained from the office of the superintendent or the principal. Annual reports are usually published in booklet form and should be obtained if available. If necessary, simply give students the necessary information and have them record it in the proper spaces on the project form. Be sure, however, to discuss the sources and significance of such information. Emphasize the social value of schools to the individual and to the community. 

No. 9. A limited uniformity of answers may be expected, but the greater the variety the better. Inspect for penmanship, neatness, and degree of completion. Both superior and average students ought to be required to write a brief statement as directed at the bottom of the form for this project. Stress the importance of good penmanship.

No. 10. Individual answers, except those about population, which will be uniform. A very brief answer to the first question is acceptable. (Natural transportation facilities, such as navigable waters, accessible raw materials, natural advantages of location, power supply, etc., are common factors in explaining the locations. of cities.) Booklets and other publicity matter of local chambers of commerce, census reports, etc. will be helpful in answering a number of the questions that constitute this project. Approximate answers for "country or district from which shipped" should be accepted as satisfactory. Do not expect too much in the way of specifying what four local business men or women have done for the community.

No. 11. Individual answers. Inspect for penmanship, neatness, and degree of completion only. See IV-A of the lesson plan for the fourteenth class period (page 76). Some preliminary explanation and discussion may be necessary in order to get students properly started on this project. Emphasize appreciations. Do not expect many students to fill out the form for this project completely. 

No. 12. Individual answers. Call attention to the classified sections of the local telephone and city directories as sources of information for working out this project. Inspect for penmanship, neatness, and degree of completion, but do not grade. Use completed projects as the basis for class discussions, comparisons, etc. 

Note: Telephone companies in most communities will gladly supply enough copies of the local telephone directory for class use. A copy of the local city directory will usually be found in the principal's office, or the publishers may even be prevailed upon to donate a copy for addition to the class library. Insist upon the correct use of the classified section of the telephone directory. This is practice in the use of reference material. Hold the superior students to the satisfactory completion of the project, but do not expect all students to come up to the standard set for the brightest ones. 

No. 13. Individual answers. Do not inspect, check, or grade.

Explain in advance of its assignment that this project is strictly confidential and that it will be neither seen nor discussed by anyone except the student, who prepares it for himself.

No. 14. Individual responses, unless the teacher desires to have all students copy some particular code. (The code of the Rotary 

Club is an excellent one for the purpose of this project.) See II-C of the lesson plan for Unit 5 (page 81). Emphasize attitudes. Do not be surprised if some students respond very briefly. 

No. 15. Unless worked out in class, answers will be individual. 

Suggestion: Perhaps the most effective treatment of this project is to work it out in class. Under the guidance and direction of the teacher, the required information can then be recorded in the proper spaces. 

Inspect briefly for penmanship, neatness, and degree of completion. 

Observe the consistent and persistent use of social-science methods in the development of business and economic information. 

No. 16. Within certain limitations, answers will be uniform. 

Inspect, check, and grade for the first nine items, in so far as students copy from pages 54–56 in the textbook. See VI-A-1-d of the lesson plan for Unit 5 (page 85). Check the names of the local school organizations. If there are many such, limit the students to the names of five or six, if you think best.

Note: In this, as in all other projects, insist that all students work as far as they are able, and emphasize the fact that only such projects as are completed in full are entitled to an A grade. 

No. 17. Individual answers. See IV-B-4 of the lesson plan for 

Unit 5 (page 83). Inspect for penmanship and neatness only.

Emphasize attitudes in the class discussion of this project. Broaden the students' vocabulary by devoting a few minutes of the recitation period to a discussion of the meaning of some of the more difficult words on the form.

No. 18. If worked out independently by each student, answers will be individual. The project may be worked out in class, however, in which case answers will be uniform. 

Suggestion: List on the blackboard the names of twenty-five or thirty famous Americans, either living or dead, and assign students the task of finding out something about their education. It may be a desirable plan to assign to each student the names of three or four famous Americans and, from the information so collected, to have the project filled out by the class working as a group. This will reduce the work required both of the individual student and of the teacher.

No. 19. Individual answers. Inspect for penmanship, neatness, and degree of completion, but do not check or grade. Do not be surprised if some students do not get interviews except with members of their immediate families. Let the individual student's interest be his guide. Refer to IV-B-9 in the lesson plan for 

Unit 6 (page 89).

Suggestion: For one of the interviews, the entire class may interview the teacher. Encourage students to ask many questions about duties. 

Such projects as this constitute effective guidance. 

No. 20. See No. 19 above. These two projects start students to thinking about jobs, essential qualifications, etc. Interviews with teachers, fellow students, parents, personal friends, etc. should be accepted as satisfactory for the purposes of such projects. 

Note: In many schools much emphasis is placed upon definite courses in "occupations." In such situations, it is well to tie up projects like 

Nos. 19 and 20 with such courses. If no such courses are offered, the projects are all the more valuable, although a reduction in emphasis on them may be desirable. 

No. 21. Refer to the suggestions for Project No. 2 above and also to IV-B-10 of the lesson plan for Unit 6 (page 89). 

No. 22. Refer to the suggestions for Project No. 4 above and also to IV-B-10 of the lesson plan for Unit 6 (page 89). As a part of the discussion of this project, require several students to defend any changes of ideas they may have had since the completion of Unit 2. 

Note: Avoid too great pressure upon students to make definite decisions. 

The purpose is to get them to begin thinking about their futures and to want to decide about them in a businesslike way. We know that the best decisions of today may be changed tomorrow. 

No. 23. Individual answers. See VI-A-2-e of the lesson plan for Unit 6 (page 90). Help students to fill in the names of their various school subjects and to record their grades. The important feature of this project is the self-analysis required. It also provides some elementary training in the preparation of graphs. Inspect, but do not check or grade.

No. 24. Individual answers. Inspect for penmanship, neatness, and degree of completion, but do not check or grade. Refer to 

VI-A-2-f of the lesson plan for Unit 6 (page 91). 

No. 25. Individual answers.

Inspect but do not attempt to check or grade. Refer to VI-A-2-ƒ of the lesson plan for Unit 6 

(page 91). 

Note: This project and all others in which guidance and vocational selection are featured need be emphasized only to the extent that guidance is featured in the school.

No. 26. Individual answers, unless the teacher decides to assign the project to a class officer or to a committee of superior students who will report back to the class. If this is done, answers will be uniform. If each student works out the project on his own initiative, an interview with a parent or personal friend is all that is necessary. The entire class might even interview the teacher. 

The underlying purpose is to awaken the student to a state of what might be called "vocational consciousness." Refer to IV-B-4 of the lesson plan for Unit 7 (page 94).

No. 27. Inspect for neatness and degree of completion, but do not check or grade. Before assigning this project, explain to the class that it is a confidential document. Emphasize the importance of desirable character traits and personal efficiency. Refer to 

IV-B-4 of the lesson plan for Unit 7 (page 94). 

Note: It is more effective clerical practice to train students to follow directions, to look up information, to get facts from outside sources, and to record data in prescribed form and arrangement than it is to have them engage in constant repetitive practice in the preparation of great quantities of common business papers. The method employed in this course is simply a little different, and the emphasis is still upon attitudes and appreciations. Technical knowledges and skills will be developed later in the natural course of events. 

No. 28. Individual answers. However, this project may be handled in the same manner as No. 26, in which case answers will be uniform. Inspect for penmanship, neatness, and degree of completion. At the discretion of the teacher, the completed project may be checked by an interchange of papers during the reci-

tation period and then graded by the teacher. Refer to IV-B-4 of the lesson plan for Unit 7 (page 94).

Note: This is one of the four or five projects in the entire course that suggests outside interviews, investigations, etc. Do not be surprised if students fail to get all the data called for in this project. Some business organizations do not like to give out such information. 

No. 29. Individual answers. This project is a challenge to the student's ability to follow directions and to his judgment. Emphasize appreciations. Set high standards for the superior group of students, but be sure also to set standards that the other groups can reach. Encourage every student to do as much work on each project as his ability, interest, and time will permit. 

No. 30. Individual answers, unless the teacher wishes to select help-wanted advertisements from one paper and thus have uniform answers. Refer to IV-B-4 of the lesson plan for Unit 7 (page 94). 

Note: One of the important outcomes of this project will be the realization that there are few desirable employment opportunities for young people under eighteen years of age who are not high school graduates. 

In other words, it pays to remain in school until education, training, and maturity are sufficient to give reasonable promise of success. 

No. 31. Individual answers. Emphasize handwriting, neatness, correct expression, form, arrangement, etc. Do not expect to receive many model business letters. The ability to write good business letters is none too common even among well-educated adults. 

No. 32. Individual answers. Refer to IV-B-4 of the lesson plan for Unit 7 (page 95). Inspect for neatness and degree of completion, but do not check or grade. 

Nos. 33, 34, and 35. Uniform answers. Inspect for penmanship, neatness, degree of completion, etc., and check for accuracy of detail. Do not expect slow students to complete all these forms, but insist upon accuracy in what they do complete. Refer to 

I-C-1 and IV-B-5 of the lesson plan for Unit 9 (pages 101–104). 

Important: It is not the purpose at this time to teach the legal implications of the documents that constitute the working materials for these three projects. It is enough if students learn that there are such business

documents as articles of incorporation, stock certificates, etc. and understand in general the purpose served by each. The most important out-come is the increased ability to follow directions. Many clerical workers handle business documents, the legal implications of which they do not. understand.

No. 36. Individual answers, unless the teacher prefers to furnish a list of business concerns to be used by each student in working out the project. If personal interviews are not practicable or desirable, much of the required information can be obtained from the local telephone or city directory. In many cases, the name of a business concern will indicate its type of organization or ownership. 

Refer to IV-B-5 of the lesson plan for Unit 9 (page 104). 

No. 37. To the extent that they are based on the Declaration of 

Independence, answers will be uniform. However, students may select different items therefrom, and to that extent answers will differ. Refer to IV-B-4 of the lesson plan for Unit 10 (page 109). 

The following are some of the sentences taken from the Declaration, from which students may make their selections: 

Immigration and Naturalization. He (referring to George III, King: 

of England) has endeavored to prevent the population of these States; for that purpose obstructing the Laws for Naturalization of Foreigners; refusing to pass others to encourage their migrations higher, and raising the conditions of new appropriations of lands. 

Laws. He has obstructed the Administration of Justice, by refusing his Assent of Laws for establishing Judiciary Powers. 

Courts. He has made Judges dependent on his will alone, for the tenure of their offices, and the amount and payment of their salaries. 

Government Officers. He has erected a multitude of New Offices, and sent hither swarms of officers to harass our people, and eat out their substance.

Jurisdiction. He has combined with others to subject us to a jurisdiction foreign to our constitution, and unacknowledged by our laws; giving his Assent to their Acts of pretended Legislation. 

Trade. For cutting off our Trade with all parts of the world. 

Taxes. For imposing Taxes on us without our Consent. 

Taking Away Charter. For taking away our charters, abolishing our most valuable Laws, and altering fundamentally the Forms of our 

Governments.

Invasion. He has plundered our seas, ravaged our Coasts, burnt our towns, and destroyed the lives of our people. 

Note: This project is a distinct challenge to the student's judgment and discrimination. The Declaration should be explained as an organized protest of colonial business men against interference with their affairs by the British Government. Do not be surprised if a number of students have difficulty in deciding which clauses of the Declaration refer specifically to business. Inspect for penmanship and accuracy of quotation, but do not grade.

To the extent that they are based on selected references to the Constitution of the United States, answers will be uniform. 

Inspect for penmanship, and check for accuracy of quotation. Any of the preceding references to the Constitution should be accepted as satisfactory.

No. 39. Individual answers. Inspect for penmanship, neatness, and degree of completion, but do not check or grade. Emphasize the aim of this project by referring to I-C-3 of the lesson plan for Unit 10 (page 107). Make this project the basis of a discussion because of its interest value No. 40. Individual answers, unless the teacher prefers to have the project worked out in class, in which case answers may be uniform. Emphasize the aim of this project by referring to I-C-4 

of the lesson plan for Unit 10 (page 107). The following govern ment employees are typical of those that may be named in this project: 

National: United States Postmaster, mail carrier, Federal judges 

United States District Attorneys, soldiers, sailors, etc.

State: Governor, Secretary of State, State Treasurer, state school officers, etc.

County: County judges, sheriff, treasurer, county clerk, recorder of deeds, farm agents, etc. 

City: Mayor, councilmen, teachers, policemen, board of health officials, firemen, etc.

Some of the buildings that may be mentioned are as follows: 

National Government: United States Post Office, United States Customs Building, United States Army or Navy buildings, Federal penitentiaries, United States hospitals, etc.

State Government: State Capitol buildings, state universities and colleges, state reformatories, penitentiaries, hospitals, etc. 

County Government: Court houses, jails, county hospitals, county farm buildings, etc.

City Government: City Hall, public school buildings, fire and police stations, city hospitals, etc.

No. 41. Individual answers, but may be checked and graded if the teacher so desires. Local conditions will determine the responses. However, almost every kind of business concern must

secure a license, franchise, or some other kind of local, state, or national permit or approval, or is subject to some kind of official inspection. Some of the business concerns that need the services. of licensed employees are drug stores, insurance companies, public accounting firms, etc.

No. 42. Uniform answers. (This project is based specifically on the subject matter found on pages 175–182 of the text.) It may be checked for accuracy in order to determine how carefully each student has studied the subject matter on which it is based. The whole project is a fine test of the students' ability to follow directions. Refer to IV-B-4 of the lesson plan for Unit 11 (pages 115-116).

Note: Like many of the others, this project may be checked very quickly by having students exchange papers. 

No. 43. Individual answers. Inspect for penmanship, neatness, and degree of completion, but do not check or grade. Refer to 

IV-B-4 of the lesson plan for Unit 11 (page 115). This and a few other projects suggest interviews with employers, visits to business houses, etc. If local conditions or school, regulations make such student activities undesirable, students may be instructed to interview home-room teachers, vocational counselors, etc. Such projects are extremely valuable, but discretion in their use is very important.

No. 45. Refer to the comments on Projects Nos. 4 and 22 above.

Do not attempt to grade the students' work on these guidance projects. Remember that each individual student is always in the process of "finding himself." It is well to discuss such projects when completed. 

Nos. 46-54 Inclusive. Completed projects will be uniform and may be graded if the teacher so desires. Refer to I-C-1 (page 119), IV-B-4 (page 121), and VI-C-2 (page 123) of the lesson plan for 

Unit 12.

Note: It is particularly important to keep in mind that students are not expected to understand the legal aspects of the documents on which the projects for this unit are based. All that should be expected is that studen s should know the purpose of, for example, a deed, a mortgage, or a will, and under what circumstances each is used. Such knowledge and the increased ability to follow directions to th letter are the essential outcomes of this series of projects. Any attempt to teach the legal implications of this series of documents will be just so much wasted effort. 

No. 55. Refer to the comments on Project No. 23 above.

Inspect for penmanship, neatness, and degree of completion, but do not attempt to check or grade.

No. 56. Individual answers. The project may be worked out in class, however, in which case a certain degree of uniformity may be achieved. Refer to I-C-1 in the lesson plan for Unit 13 (page 126). Reference to a standard text on geography will be helpful in working out this project. The students should be shown how to estimate the approximate distances by use of the scale of miles which accompanies practically every map.
