No. 58. Answers will be fairly uniform. If booklets containing local traffic regulations are available, be sure to have copies brought to class. Such booklets may be obtained from the local police department, the chamber of commerce, or some other organization interested in public safety. A few moments' interview with a local traffic officer, either by the teacher or by some student helper, will supply most of the information required in working out this project. Perhaps the answers may be worked out by class discussion.

Note: This project is more than a lesson in safety or citizenship. It represents a businesslike use of local traffic information and regulations. 

Motormen, drivers, chauffeurs, and automobile owners will find that such knowledge is worth-while business information. 

No. 59. Uniform answers. The completed projects should be inspected, checked, and graded very carefully. Perhaps the

teacher will want to assign certain students as helpers for this purpose.

Important: Observe the challenge to the student's pride in his ability to put to practical use such arithmetical skill as he may possess. Most of the items on the second and third sheets of this project are really arithmetic problems, although we must be careful not to describe them as such. These practical problems or situations cannot be solved unless the student follows directions, which is good clerical practice. Neither can they be solved unless the student refers to the textbook, which is more good clerical practice. Neither can they be solved unless the student refers to and interprets a time-table, which is still more good clerical practice. Finally, they cannot be solved unless the student puts to practical use his knowledge of arithmetic. 

More Important: Undoubtedly most teachers will want to localize this entire project by securing time-tables of local railroads. If that is done, the situations given on the second and third sheets will, of course, have to be changed accordingly, and a few of them may not be altogether appropriate to local conditions (changes in time, etc.). Observe, too, that a project of this kind may be expanded almost indefinitely. 

If the project is based on the time-table reproduced on the first sheet, the following solutions may be used in checking the accuracy of the students' finished work:

Sheet No. 2 

"Give the number and time of departure from Chicago of all trains on which a passenger could travel from that city to Detroit.' No. 56, 12:05 a.m.; No. 46, 1:50 a.m.; No. 2, 6:45 a.m.; No. 30, 8:00 a.m.; No. 44, 9:00 a.m.; No. 10, 10:30 a.m.; No. 8, 11:00 a.m.; No. 4, 12:00 p.m.; 

No. 14, 3:00 p.m.; No. 26, 3:30 p.m. "Give the number of each of these trains that runs through to Buffalo." 

Nos. 56, 30, 44, 10, 8, 14, 40. "Give the numbers of trains that run only from Chicago to Kalamazoo.” 

No. 42. "Give the numbers of those that run only from Jackson to Detroit." 

Nos. 102, 108. "Give the numbers and time of departure from Detroit of those that run only from that city to Buffalo." No. 2, 6:05 a.m.; No. 36, 2:30 p.m. "Give the numbers and time of departure from Chicago of the four trains that leave Chicago during the night and arrive in Detroit the next

morning." No. 56, 12:05 a.m.; No. 46, 1:50 a.m.; No. 6, 10:00 p.m.; 

No. 16, 11:45 p.m. "How many trains make all stops between Chicago and Detroit?" 

No. 2 only. "Could a passenger travel on train No. 40 from Michigan City, Indiana, to Kalamazoo, Michigan? Why?" No, because it has no Pullman accommodation for local intermediate passengers. "Train No. 8 leaves Ann Arbor at 4 p.m.; train No. 10 leaves at 4:40 p.m. On which of these two trains would a passenger travel from Ann 

Arbor to Buffalo? Why?" No. 10, because train No. 8 stops on signal for passengers for New York, Boston, and Philadelphia when Pullman space is available. "Which trains from Chicago to Detroit have no accommodation for day-coach passengers?" No. 8. "After 8:45 p.m. a resident of Kalamazoo is in Chicago and wants to get home as soon as possible. Train No. 40 leaves at 9 p.m. and No. 6 leaves at 10 p.m. Which train will he take? Why?" No. 40, because it arrives earlier, or shortly after midnight. It is also a better train. 

Sheet No. 3 

Leave Chicago at 8:00 a.m. on train No. 30; arrive Jackson at 12:30 p.m. 

Leave Jackson at 3:15 p.m. on train No. 8; arrive at Detroit at 4:45 p.m. 

Central time, or 5:45 Eastern time. "How many minutes actually elapse between the arrival and departure of train No. 20 from Detroit?" 20 minutes. (N.B. change of time.) "How far is it from Chicago to Detroit?" 283.5 miles. “To Buffalo?” 

535.2 miles. "From Michigan City, Indiana, to Battle Creek, Michigan?" 

108.3 miles. "When it is noon in Chicago, what time is it in Buffalo?" 1:00 p.m. "In Jackson, Michigan?" Noon. "When it is noon in Buffalo, what time is it in Kalamazoo?" 11:00a.m. "In hours and minutes, what is the actual elapsed running time of train 

No. 8 between Chicago and Buffalo? (Do not overlook the difference between Central and Eastern time.)" 10 hrs. 35 min. "An extra charge is made for the privilege of riding on several trains on this schedule. Give the numbers of such trains." Nos. 30, 44, 8, 4, 26, 20, 40. "Why is this extra charge made?" Faster running time. "Does it include the cost of a parlor-car seat or a berth in a sleeping car?" No. (See pages 236 and 237 of text. Special tickets must be purchased for a parlor-car seat or a berth in a sleeping car.)

"Assuming that a passenger were ready to leave Chicago at any time after 9:30 p.m. and wanted to be in Detroit by 8:30 a.m., on which of these trains would he probably prefer to travel? Why?" No. 16 leaving at 11:45 p.m., because it does not make any stops. "Does train No. 30 arrive in Kalamazoo in the morning or in the afternoon?" In the morning. "In Jackson?" In the afternoon. "A passenger leaves Chicago on Monday, traveling on train No. 10 bound for Buffalo. On what day will he arrive at his destination? Will he arrive in the morning or in the afternoon?" On Tuesday morning.

No. 60. Uniform answers. This project should be inspected, checked, and graded. Students should be held to a high standard of accuracy. Suggestions for Project No. 59 also apply here.

Sheet No. 2 

"In the proper spaces below, give (a) the time of departure from 

Detroit and arrival at Cincinnati on Friday, (b) the time of departure from Cincinnati and arrival at Pittsburgh on Monday, and (c) the running time for each trip. Friday

Monday 

8:00 a.m. a. Leave Cincinnati. b. Arrive Pittsburgh.. c. Running time.. a. Leave Detroit..... b. Arrive Cincinnati... 7:00 p.m. c. Running time.. 

11 hours. 

8:00 a.m. 

8:00 p.m. 

12 hours. 

Scheduled stops between Detroit and Cincinnati: Toledo, Findlay, and Dayton. Passengers can get lunch at Findlay and Dayton. 

Scheduled stops between Cincinnati and Pittsburgh: Dayton, Columbus, and Cambridge, Ohio, and Wheeling, West Virginia. Passengers can get something to eat at Columbus, Ohio, and at Wheeling, West Virginia. 

The trip from Cincinnati to Pittsburgh takes more time than the trip from Detroit to Cincinnati.

The Detroit business man would not be likely to make the trip to Cincinnati by night bus. He would probably travel by train, using 

Pullman sleeping-car service. It would be more comfortable for him to travel that way. For overnight travel, the Pullman sleeping car is generally preferred because of its greater comfort. 

Undoubtedly, the state of Ohio has an excellent system of good roads. 

Otherwise, motor busses would not operate between so many of the cities and towns.

Note: This project may well be localized by securing local bus schedules, which will make it more interesting and more practicable.

Poland "Which of the five vessels operated by this steamship line makes the fastest time?" S. S. "Leviathan." "Why do you think the rates for first-class accommodations are higher in summer than in winter?" More people are traveling. "How long does it take a vessel of this line to make the journey from 

Plymouth to Bremen?" One day. "What is the minimum cost of second-class accommodations on the 

S. S. 'Leviathan' from New York to Cherbourg?" $152.50 plus $5, or $157.50. "On which of the vessels operated by this steamship line are traveling accommodations of most classes more expensive than corresponding accommodations on the other vessels?" S. S. "Leviathan."

"Judging from the schedules of sailing dates and minimum rates, two vessels operated by this line offer faster and more luxurious service than the others. Consult the schedules and name these two vessels." 

S. S. "Leviathan" and "George Washington." "State your reason for your answer to the previous question." The schedule of rates on these two vessels is higher for both summer and winter. 

No. 62. Uniform answers. This project should be inspected, checked, and graded. Insist upon a high degree of accuracy. 

See suggestions for Projects Nos. 59, 60, and 61. The solution follows: 

Sheet No. 2 

"What is the total cost of traveling by train from New York (a) to 

Columbus, Ohio?" $32.68; "(b) to St. Louis, Missouri?" $53.74. "How far is it from New York to Columbus?" 630 miles. "From 

Columbus to St. Louis?" 408 miles. "From New York to St. Louis?" 

1,038 miles. "How long does it take to make the journey from New York to St. 

Louis traveling all the way by train?" 24 hours, 5 minutes. (St. Louis is on Central time.) "How much time is saved by leaving the train at Columbus Airport and traveling the rest of the way to St. Louis by airplane?" 5 hours, 7 minutes. (24 hours, 5 minutes minus 18 hours, 58 minutes equals 5 hours, 7 minutes.) "What is the total cost of the journey from New York to St. Louis, using the combined train-plane service?" $53.43. "What is the cost per mile of railroad fare, not including other charges of the trip, between New York City and Columbus? (In making your calculation, carry the division far enough so that your answer will be given in cents and mills, thus: $.089, which means eight cents and nine mills, or 8.9 cents.)" $.036 per mile. "What is the cost per mile of the airplane trip from Columbus to St. 

Louis?" 5.15 cents per mile. "The distances between New York City and Columbus, and between Columbus and Kansas City are almost the same. In hours and minutes, how long does it take to make the trip from New York City to Columbus by train?" 13 hours, 50 minutes. "How long does it take to make the trip from Columbus to Kansas 

City by airplane? (Do not forget the change from Eastern to Central time.)" 7 hours, 32 minutes.

"What is the distance between Los Angeles and San Francisco?" 380 miles. "How long does it take to make the journey between these two cities by airplane?" 3 hours, 30 minutes. "How far is it from Clovis, New Mexico, to Los Angeles by air?" 

897 miles. "It takes almost 10 hours to travel from Clovis, New Mexico, to 

Los Angeles by air. What is the average rate of speed per hour?" 89.7 miles per hour. "On a transcontinental train-plane trip, what is the total distance between New York City and Los Angeles?" 2,727 miles. "How much of this distance is covered by train?" 918 miles. "How much by airplane?" 1,809 miles. "When it is noon in New York City, what time is it in St. Louis?" 

11:00 a.m. "In Albuquerque?" 10:00 a.m. "In Los Angeles?" 9:00 a.m. "In San Francisco?" 9:00 a.m. "When it is 11:00 a.m. in San Francisco, what time is it in St. Louis?" 

1:00 p.m. "In New York City?" 2:00 p.m. "Are the Pennsylvania and Santa Fe Railroads and the Transcontinental Air Transport Company obligated to maintain the schedule of rates quoted on the first sheet for this project for any definite length of time?" No, rates are subject to change without notice.

Note: Both rate and time schedules of the kind used as the basis for this project change very frequently. Although such changes do not materially affect the value of the project, no doubt many teachers will want to obtain copies of current schedules and to localize the project. 

When this is done, the questions on the second sheet will be suggestive only and will need adjustment to local and current conditions. Most transportation companies will gladly furnish time-tables, schedules, etc. in sufficient quantities so that each student may have copies. One thing to emphasize about such projects as this and the four preceding ones is that time and rate schedules change frequently, which makes it necessary to keep one's information about such matters up to date.

Note: Observe that while Project No. 64 is required, No. 65 is optional. 

No doubt, many students will decide that they do not care to investigate employment opportunities in the field of transportation. It is just as important for them to decide that this field does not appeal to them as it is for other students to decide that they would like to work in the field of transportation.

No. 71. Uniform answers for classes of local telephone service and rates. Data about long-distance charges will also be uniform if the teacher designates some particular city. Usually, all the information required for working out this project may be obtained from the local telephone directory. If such is not the case, however, the proper telephone operator will give complete information. Some student helper may be assigned the responsibility of obtaining the information. Obviously, the project cannot be keyed because it is localized. 

No. 72. Individual answers unless the teacher prefers to standardize the responses by means of class discussion. Refer to pages

325-327 of the text;

also to IV-B-3 of the lesson plan for Unit 17 (page 155). 

No. 73. Uniform answers. The project should be thoroughly discussed in class before it is assigned to be worked out. Inspect, check, and grade for the items you consider most important. 

Obviously, the project cannot be keyed because it calls for localized information.

No. 80. Uniform answers. A copy of a daily paper in the hands of each student will motivate this project and make it more real. 

If enough copies of the paper cannot be secured, the financial section of a single copy may be posted on the bulletin board so that every student can get the necessary data from it at his convenience. Unless the newspaper is used in some way, this project will have little value other than the applied arithmetic that is involved.

No. 81. Individual responses will consist of the different financial bulletins obtained by students. If only one copy is obtained by some class officer, it should be posted on the bulletin board. The project is not vitally important, and may be omitted entirely at the discretion of the teacher.

No. 82. Uniform answers. The deposit slips should be inspected and checked. In order to make this project more real, certain student helpers may be assigned the responsibility of inspecting the written work of other members of the class or of acting as receiving tellers in accepting the deposits. Note: Compare the bank statement used as the basis of this project with the one illustrated on page 394 of the text, also the reconciliation on the project form with the one illustrated on page 396 of the text. Although there are a number of differences in form, arrangement, etc., they serve the same purpose.

Whenever possible in connection with such projects, have students collect similar forms and bring them to class. In this way, the students' study of bank statements will be localized.

No. 87. Uniform answers. Therefore, check and grade, paying particular attention to penmanship, neatness, accuracy, etc. 

No. 88. Uniform answers. Therefore, check and grade. In all these projects insist upon good handwriting, neatness, and accuracy. Expect the most from the brightest students, a little less from average students, and still less from slow students. 

No. 89. Uniform answers for the first part of the telegraph form. 

The second part will not be uniform. However, the following is one way in which the message might have been worded: 

RETURN IMMEDIATELY VIA SANTA FE AND NEW YORK CENTRAL LOVE 

No. 90. Uniform answers. Therefore, check and grade. 

Suggestion: Have the printed matter at the bottom of this collateral note read by the class, and discuss its meaning briefly. Do not expect too much in the way of comprehension of the legal implications of such documents. It is enough if students understand when such notes are used. 

No. 91. Uniform answers. Therefore, check and grade. Perhaps it would be well to post some of the neatest notes on the bulletin board.

No. 97. Individual answers. However, the solutions may be made uniform if the teacher cares to obtain, or have a student helper obtain, the information, which may then be recorded uniformly by the entire class. Refer to I-C-1 of the lesson plan for Unit 20 (page 176). 

No. 98. Individual answers. Therefore, do not attempt to check or grade. Have student helpers or class officers assist in this project. If only one or two advertisements are obtained, mount them on the bulletin board. This is one of the less important projects. 

No. 99. Individual answers. Inspect for penmanship, neatness, and degree of completion, but do not attempt to check or grade. 

Insist that students confine their writing to the ruled spaces, which is good clerical and good prebookkeeping training. 

No. 100. Individual answers. Inspect, but do not check or grade. See comments on Project No. 99 above. Do not accept any poor work.

No. 101. Individual answers. Inspect for penmanship and neatness, and check in order to make sure that every item of information is supplied. If a question requires no answer, have students write the word "None" in the space provided. The

checking may be done by exchanging papers or by student helpers, thus relieving the teacher of burdensome details. 

Suggestion: If two or three student helpers or class officers can do so, have them get actual application blanks from insurance agents. 

No. 102. Individual answers. Refer to preceding comments on 

Projects Nos. 4, 64, 76, and 95; also to IV-A-1 of the lesson plan for Unit 21 (page 182). 

No. 103. Individual answers. Refer to preceding comments on Projects Nos. 26, 28, 65, and 96; also to IV-A-2 of the lesson plan for Unit 21 (page 182).

No. 104. Individual answers. Inspect for penmanship, neatness, degree of completion, etc. There will probably be a considerable variety of answers to this project, unless it is worked out in class and students are instructed as to United States and foreign sources of the food products mentioned. Instruct students to use the words "local" and "imported" wherever possible in working out this project. 

No. 105. Individual answers. Therefore, inspect for penmanship, neatness, and degree of completion. Suggest that students use some standard geography text as a source of information. 

Do not be too technical, because business conditions change constantly and frequently. The purpose of the project is to develop a desirable attitude toward business and an appreciation of manufacturing.

Suggestion: Impress upon students that everything they have learned in other courses has some bearing upon the field of business. Show them that the duties of a petty clerical job do not present an adequate picture of the great field of business. "Business is the social agency that supplies people with want-satisfying goods and worth-while services." 

No. 106. Individual answers. However, the project may be worked out by the class as a group and uniform answers reported on the form. The tables on page 252 are suggestive of the answers that may be expected to the first two sections of the project. 

Localized data are required for Section 3. The entire project should be inspected for penmanship, neatness, and degree of completion, and Section 3 should be checked carefully for accuracy of names and addresses of local concerns.

Furniture No. 107. Uniform answers. 

Clothing 

Furniture 

Household appliances 

Therefore, inspect and check.

It will be more satisfactory to work out this project in class. 

With the exception of the first four items listed in the left-hand column, most of the businesses mentioned except the grocery store, the department store, and the public school do all the things that a factory does. However, railroads, telephone companies, and city water works do not manufacture their own products. 

The canning factory and the automobile factory do all the things mentioned in the form. The grocery store, the railroad, the telephone company, the department store, the public school, and the city water works do not engage in manufacturing operations.

No. 110. Individual answers for the first three sections of this project. 

Section 4. Largest stocks of straw hats, during May, June, and

July; of washable silk dresses, during late spring and summer months; men's overcoats, during fall and early winter months; furs, from August to February inclusive.

August; men's overcoats, usually during late winter months; washable silk dresses, during late summer months; furs, during late winter months, also by some stores during August.

No. 111. Individual answers unless the teacher prefers to have the project worked out in class, in which case answers may be brought into uniformity. The following suggestions may be indefinitely expanded both by teacher and student: 

1. Items purchased for a railroad: ties, poles, steel rails, spikes, slag, gravel, engines, passenger cars, coal, water, oil, paint, concrete, wood, tools, paper, tickets, etc. 

2. Things purchased for a bank: paper, cards, books, small banks, pencils, pens, ink, paint, typewriters, adding machines, bookkeeping machines, furniture, advertising booklets, etc. 

3. Things purchased by a shoe factory: leather, cloth, nails, thread, dye, boxes, machinery, etc. 

4. Things purchased by a grocery store: flour, sugar, coffee, tea, spices, canned fruit, canned vegetables, fresh fruit, fresh vegetables, dried fruit, butter, eggs, cheese, soap, brooms, scales, counters, paper pads, wrapping paper, paper bags, etc. 

5. Things purchased by a hotel: linen, bedding, silver, carpets, furniture, cleaning materials, paper, pens, ink, etc. 

6. Things purchased by a children's department: toys, underthings, jackets, dresses, rompers, stockings, shoes, rubbers, sweaters, hats, caps, soap, towels, bedding, blankets, etc.

7. Things purchased by a grocery store: scales, counters, wrapping paper, paper bags, twine, soap, brooms, mops, brushes, etc.

Inspect completed projects for penmanship, neatness, and degree of completion, and check for accuracy of items listed.

No. 134. Individual answers. However, the project may be worked out in class and thus made uniform. 

No. 135. Uniform answers. Insist upon a high degree of accuracy and neatness. The completed project should be inspected, but can only be graded subjectively. 

No. 136. Individual answers. If a trip to some business house is impracticable, a brief visit to the principal's office will be sufficient. Still another alternative is to have some student describe a filing system that he has observed in some local business office. 

Representatives of filing equipment manufacturers can often be prevailed upon to address classes. 

No. 137. Individual answers. However, the following responses are suggestive of those that may be expected: 

1. What Father Took: cattle, horses, ammunition, tools, wagon, weapons. 

What the Boy Took: tools, ammunition, weapons, traps, dog, fishhooks. 

What the Girl Took: sewing basket, cloth, sewing equipment, clothing, doll. 

What Mother Took: sewing equipment, cooking equipment, spinning equipment, washing equipment, bedding, cloth. 

2. Animals and birds shot for food: deer, bear, buffalo, rabbit, turkey, grouse, quail. 

3. Animals used for furs and skins: deer, beaver, muskrat, buffalo, mink, bear, squirrel. 

4. Wild foodstuffs: raspberries, blackberries, strawberries, hickory nuts, walnuts, beechnuts, grapes. 

5. Crops planted by the pioneer: corn, potatoes, beans, pumpkins, oats, wheat. 

6. Things made by pioneer housewife: yarn, thread, clothing, knitted materials, dried and canned foods. 

7. Things made by pioneer father: furniture, harness, log house, tools, tanned skins. 

8. Things traded for at trading posts: salt, sugar, powder, weapons, tools, implements, cloth, traps, flint. 

9. Things traded to the Indians: blankets, weapons, gunpowder, beads, trinkets. 

10. Things traded to pioneers by Indians: wampum, corn, skins, furs.

11. Things traded to Indians by traders: weapons, gunpowder, trinkets, beads, medicine, clothing. 

12. Early trading posts in the United States: Astoria, Oregon; St. 

Joseph, Missouri; Chicago, Illinois; Pittsburgh, Pennsylvania; Boonesboro, Kentucky.

Note: A number of inventions have been included in the above list about which little information is obtainable. In such cases, question marks have been inserted in the solution. The underlying purpose of the

project is to emphasize the fact that inventions result in manufacturing operations, which, in turn, means increased comforts and conveniences for more people.

They may be graded at the discretion of the teacher, but, in the opinion of the authors, grading is not essential.
